Google commits €13bn to Finnish digital infrastructure
2026-09-09

Google commits €13bn to Finnish digital infrastructure

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Google's Hamina data centre. Image: Google Once operational, Google said the new infrastructure will support 7,000 jobs annually. In its largest single investment in Europe to date, Google has promised to pump €13bn into Finland’s digital infrastructure over two years, including supporting data centre build-outs and other strategic infrastructure, while also promising to fund clean energy and nature restoration projects. During its initial construction phase next year, this investment is expected to support more than 37,000 jobs nationwide in Finland and contribute an annual average of €3.6bn to the country’s GPD, Google said. Around 16,000 of these jobs are estimated to be in construction. Once operational, Google said the new infrastructure will support 7,000 jobs annually, including on-site facility, technical roles and upstream suppliers of equipment and services. The technology juggernaut is partnering with Fingrid – Finland’s national grid operator – and Business Finland to identity locations for its new infrastructure. The new data centres can be expected in the north of the country where existing grid infrastructure near carbon-free energy sources will help Google’s electricity consumption. Google has had a presence of more than 15 years in Finland, which started with the company converting a paper mill in Hamina into a data centre. According to the company, the Hamina data centre is designed to cover 80pc of the annual heating needs for the district. The existing Hamina data centre – a part of the company’s global network of 43 cloud regions – is used by organisations such as Nokia, Elisa and the city of Hamina and helps power services including Gemini AI, Search, Maps and YouTube. In addition, the company said it is also extending its power purchase agreement with Finnish energy company Fortum by 22 years to support its Loviisa nuclear power plant, located near Google’s Hamina data centre. The two companies have signed a memorandum of understanding to explore and advance cooperation on new flexibility capacity and new power generation capacity, including working to improve the commercial viability of potential new nuclear reactors at the Loviisa site. Meanwhile, additional deals with energy companies Valorem and Suomen Hyötytuuli brings Google’s total new-to-the-grid onshore wind capacity in Finland to nearly 630MW. The major investment will also see a 94MW battery system connected to Finland’s grid, located near Google’s site in Kajaani, which is expected to be operational in late 2027. Google said that it will invest in environmental initiatives across its data centre sites in Hamina, Kajaani, Muhos and Vaala, including focusing on nature regeneration and safeguarding ecological corridors for local wildlife. The company is investing €31m over the span of four years across its four sites to support local community initiatives focused on education and economic growth, with €10m specifically earmarked for research and innovation. The funding is also expected to support AI education programmes delivered in partnership with local vocational colleges and labour organisations. It expects to support more than 4,400 workers to equip themselves with AI skills, and train up to 100 local Finnish students over one year for new data centre careers. Compliance experts find firms lack staff skilled in security and regulation
2026-09-09

Compliance experts find firms lack staff skilled in security and regulation

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
The report found that the gap in skills is leading to increased financial risk for organisations. A new report from Ireland’s professional body for compliance professionals, the Compliance Institute, has found evidence of a growing skills gap within financial services. 51pc of the 125 senior compliance experts who contributed to the research survey said that companies lack the skilled workforce needed to stay on top of financial crimes and meet growing regulatory obligations. 82pc were of the opinion that financial services firms lacking the necessary skilled workforce are increasing their financial or operational risk, and more than half (56pc) believe Ireland is not developing enough talent in compliance and financial crime to meet future demand in the financial services sector. Michael Kavanagh, the CEO of the Compliance Institute, said, “At a time when financial crime is becoming more complex, sophisticated and global, it’s concerning that the overwhelming majority of compliance experts believe that talent shortages in the areas of financial crime and compliance are increasing regulatory and operational risks for the Irish financial services sector.” He added: “Financial crime is increasingly digital in nature, faster to execute and more complex to detect, and furthermore, AI has made the task of tackling financial crime even more challenging. Unlocking the potential of biology for patients Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions “So, it’s crucial that financial services institutions have the right firepower to combat financial crime, and a key part of this is suitably qualified staff and well-resourced compliance teams.” Kavanagh explained that it is critical that the financial services sector has consistent access to adequately skilled compliance staff, especially as the overwhelming volume and pace of regulatory changes puts pressure on compliance teams in the sector. “The sheer breadth of regulations demands a significant allocation of time, expertise and operational focus, often stretching teams to their limits. It has never been more important for the industry to have access to the right talent,” he said. He explained that companies should take the necessary steps to ensure that their staff are well-trained and possess the skills needed to meet regulatory and operational challenges. He added: “But there also needs to be a concerted effort by the Government and industry to invest more in talent so that the Irish financial services sector can truly prosper and navigate all the challenges that come its way.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Irish cyber and compliance company ZeroRisk raises $10m

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
From left: Patrick Pinschmidt, MiddleGame; Gary Nolan, ZeroRisk; and Niall McEvoy, Elkstone. Image: Chris Bellew/Fennell Photography The company’s platform aims to help payment processors and retailers manage and reduce cyber risk. Irish cybersecurity and compliance company ZeroRisk has raised $10m in a Series A funding round led by MiddleGame Ventures with participation from existing investor Elkstone. The company aims to help payment processors and retailers manage and reduce cyber risk by uniting “risk monitoring, compliance, education, guided remediation and protection services in one platform”. It offers payment processors “a single view of cyber risk across the hundreds of thousands of retailers using their payment processing technology”, and provides individual retailers with “clear guidance on the risks that matter to their business and the steps they can take to address them”, according to the company. ZeroRisk said the new funding would be used to support its expansion globally, further its product development, and increase operational capacity to deploy the platform across large merchant portfolios of major payment providers. Co-founder and CEO Gary Nolan said: “Payment companies have spent years trying to manage merchant risk through annual compliance exercises. “That is no longer enough. Their customers are dealing with scams, credential theft, website vulnerabilities and attacks on devices every day. “ZeroRisk has moved from proving the product to deploying it at scale. This investment will allow us to expand the team, deepen the platform and support the payment providers that have chosen to build their next-generation of merchant cyber programmes with us.” The company works with European and North American customers including Bank of America, Global Payments, Checkout.com and Trust Payments. Patrick Pinschmidt, a co-managing partner at MiddleGame Ventures, said: “The ZeroRisk team brings a unique combination of commercial and technical expertise, reinforced by working hand-in-hand with leading payment providers.” ZeroRisk, which was founded in 2023 and has Irish operations in Dublin and Longford, and a US office in Atlanta, said it “expects to grow revenue threefold in 2026 as contracted programmes move into deployment and its international customer base grows”. The Irish company, co-founded by Nolan, Francesco Consiglio and Marco Borza, raised $4m in February 2025 in a round led by Elkstone, whose head of venture Niall McEvoy said of the new funding: “The commercial progress in a short period of time has been impressive, and this Series A funding round further validates investor confidence that ZeroRisk can deliver transformational change in the sector globally.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Facebook Is Hosting Huge Numbers of Horrifying AI-Generated Videos of Violent Child Abuse, and Meta Is Barely Even Pretending to Care About Taking Them Down
2026-09-09

Facebook Is Hosting Huge Numbers of Horrifying AI-Generated Videos of Violent Child Abuse, and Meta Is Barely Even Pretending to Care About Taking Them Down

Futurism | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Content warning: this story contains descriptions of disturbing AI-generated videos that depict violent child abuse. As Meta has embraced AI at a deep corporate level over the past few years, its users’ feeds on Facebook and Instagram have been overrun with AI-generated material. Much of this AI content is insipid or manipulative, clearly produced at scale to take advantage of Meta’s indifference to the slop by harvesting as many human eyeballs as possible in various monetization schemes. Some of the AI material has now grown so dark that it defies easy comprehension. A Futurism investigation has uncovered a huge number of Facebook accounts uploading streams of videos depicting violent child abuse — and Meta is barely pretending to care about taking them down. Facebook accounts dedicated to the horrific content have uploaded videos of babies and young children being slapped, kicked, bludgeoned with heavy objects, dragged by their hair, burned with stoves and irons, chained up and locked in cages and freezers, thrown down stairs, threatened with knives and guns, starved, made to eat dog food and garbage off the floor, and much more. In one video, a woman screams at a little girl to stop crying. “Please don’t hit Noah, hit me instead,” the girl sobs, with cuts and bruises on her cheek and forehead, as another child cowers. In another, a visibly dirty boy begs for food. “Please ma’am, just a few dollars for food,” he implores a woman, prompting her to slap him in the face twice. He falls to the floor, bleeding from his nose. In another, a man drips wax from a burning candle onto the hands of a boy who has been tied up. “It hurts!” the boy cries, flinching in pain. Some of the cruelties in the videos are astonishing in their specificity, seemingly generated at scale using repeated prompts. Some involve withholding an inhaler from a child who needs it, while others show allergic children forced to eat shellfish. Others show adults pouring snakes and cockroaches on weeping kids. Many more involve young Black children being whipped or beaten by white adults as they perform manual labor in what looks like a colonial African setting. The videos aren’t quite photorealistic, but they are clearly being generated with cutting-edge technology, and the effect is distressing. Many commenters on the videos, which often attract thousands or even tens of thousands of reactions on Facebook, seem unaware that they’re synthetic, with many reacting in horror — or begging for a follow-up video that continues the disturbing narrative. “You psycho, why are you abusing the child,” wrote one user on a video of a woman slapping a boy in the face with a belt, leaving a bloody mark around his eye. “You deserve a punishment and a big one.” At first, we thought that we could map out the full network of accounts sharing the child abuse content. But after days of reviewing accounts and videos, we kept finding hundreds more. In effect, the supply seems to be endless, with the creators of the material uploading it as fast as we could identify it. Meta’s policy about violence against children is phrased ambiguously, saying that it bans “videos or photos that depict real or non-real non-sexual child abuse regardless of sharing intent, unless the imagery is from real-world art, cartoons, movies or video games.” The policy doesn’t clarify whether those carve-outs include AI-generated content. Contacted with questions and examples of the sickening videos, Meta offered a brief statement. “We have strict rules against content that depicts physical harm to children — whether it’s real or AI-generated — and we have removed these videos,” it read. Meta has long struggled to control the proliferation of troubling content on its platforms. Just this week, Wired reported that the company has accepted money from advertisers to run hundreds of promos for “nudify” apps that contained AI-generated sexual imagery of children, including numerous real-life minors whose photos were pilfered from social media and stock images. Provided with numerous examples of the violent child abuse content and asked whether Meta would work to proactively take the material down, the company’s spokesperson declined to comment on the record, opting only to share a Meta page about the company’s efforts to automatically remove child abuse content. The data on the page is hard to parse and doesn’t specify how many videos of violent child abuse the company removes, never mind whether they’re AI-generated. One defense the Meta spokesperson offered is that many of the videos first show violence against children before an adult steps in to protect the child. This is trivially true in many examples, but it feels more like a proactive defense by the creators of the content against attempts at moderation, or perhaps a strategy to evade guardrails on an AI video generator. And in reality, the mass-produced videos sometimes devolve into orgies of brutality in which the violence against children gives way to various adults punching and slapping each other in ways that don’t make sense. The company removed the majority of the videos we flagged to its press team, but left others online. The videos it opted not to remove included one depicting a young girl shut in a chest freezer, another showing a boy discarded in a trash can where he is seen gnawing on bones, and another of a girl in a classroom environment being forced to eat garbage. Curious about how Meta polices such vile content in practice, we conducted a test by reporting eight accounts that had been sharing AI-generated child abuse videos. Out of the eight accounts we reported, Meta eventually removed only two — and in one of those cases, it initially rejected the report before overriding the decision and taking it down. The timelines for action were lengthy, exceeding a week. “We’ve taken a look and found that the page doesn’t go against our Community Standards,” the company wrote. The six accounts that the company’s moderators left up had shared many heinous videos, including a toddler being struck in the face with a frying pan, a girl being beaten with a baseball bat, and a baby being kicked. (After a thoughtful internal discussion, we are opting not to share any of the videos at this time; if you are a member of the media considering follow-up coverage, please feel free to email us and we can provide examples to corroborate our reporting.) If Meta were serious about proactively taking down the child abuse content, or detecting it at the time of upload, it would almost certainly be able to do so. In fact, AI systems could probably be helpful: the company has invested hundreds of billions of dollars toward building “superintelligence” — reportedly making offers to top talent in the industry that have exceeded a billion dollars for a single hire — with Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg saying last year that “whoever gets there first is going to have a long-term, durable advantage towards building one of the most important products in history.” The tech to moderate these types of videos automatically already exists. In rudimentary testing, we found that Anthropic’s Claude AI was adept at evaluating whether videos contain imagery of violence against children. For that matter, Meta appears to have already deployed systems that can identify the stomach-turning videos, even if it’s declining to use them to moderate content on its platforms. We identified the vast majority of the horrid accounts simply by loading one of them, and then scrolling Facebook’s recommendation feature, which eagerly provided a bottomless feed of similar appalling material. In other words, Meta has extraordinary resources at its disposal to remove the child abuse videos, or to prevent them from being uploaded in the first place. But even when the material is flagged to it directly by journalists or reported by concerned users, it’s dragging its feet and refusing to delete it — a familiar pattern for a company that has long claimed to care about abuses of its platforms while doing little to stop them. When we presented that line of thinking to Meta, the final abhorrent videos we’d flagged to the company’s press team finally disappeared from Facebook — though untold thousands remain on its platforms. The company also provided a brief final statement. “Not all the links you sent violate our policies,” it read. “Can you please avoid suggesting in your reporting that they do?” More on Meta: Teen Boys Are Using Meta Glasses to Harass and Bully Girls at High Schools and Middle Schools

As sordid tales of AI layoffs and automation grip the news cycle, one group is fighting fire with fire, by staging an anti-AI demonstration with dozens of humanoid robots. The rally, first reported by Agence France-Presse, was organized by the Polish group Democratism. It featured some 30 robots waving flags calling for swift regulation of AI. Staged outside the offices of Poland’s Digital Affairs Ministry in Warsaw, the organizers told reporters they used the robots to demonstrate the current state of robotics and AI. Though humanoid robots remain in their infancy, the robots gathered together do make for a striking display. Around 30 robots joined a protest in front of Poland’s digital affairs ministry, calling for stronger AI regulations. The demonstration was organised by campaigners to spark debate on how AI and robots could replace workers. pic.twitter.com/QEbvchb2RS “The robots present here are meant to show that this technology is already a reality,” the organizers told AFP via an Agibot A3, which contains voice-recognition capabilities integrated with a large language model. “We particularly want to address the issue of the potential replacement of people both by humanoid robots and by artificial intelligence in cognitive work,” Democratism organizer Grzegorz Kuliś told the news service. “If we don’t react now, we may soon have a problem… these robots can already move around freely, they’re already ‘protesting.'” Notably, Kuliś is the chief information officer and owner of a company called Delta Robots, meaning the demonstration also serves to drum up press for his own commercial interests. Kuliś’ altruistic motivations notwithstanding, plenty of major AI labs like Anthropic and OpenAI have taken to using scare tactics to promote the supposedly advanced capabilities of their own products — while calling for stricter regulation of their own industry. Whether the Polish robotics wiz is following in the self-serving footsteps of Sam Altman remains to be seen. But either way, it marks the first known use of humanoid robots for a political demonstration — and it surely won’t be the last. More on robots: Humanoid Robot Fights Back After Getting Shoved

Bizarre Glitch Causes Delivery Robots to Swarm Busy City Sidewalk

The companies ushering in food delivery robots aren’t buying themselves much goodwill in the Windy City. In the short amount of time they’ve been on Chicago streets, the bots have sent residents to the hospital and smashed through bus stops, prompting local officials to set up an exclusion zone prohibiting the companies from operating in a certain section of the city. AI School Hooking Children Up to Heart Monitor in Front of Class While Playing Critiques of Their Work
2026-09-08

AI School Hooking Children Up to Heart Monitor in Front of Class While Playing Critiques of Their Work

Futurism | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Schools across the world are experimenting with AI tools, usually to the dismay of unsuspecting parents. But one privately-run school is explicitly embracing AI as part of its sales pitch, and the in-class experience sounds more bizarre than anything you’d imagine. A blistering new article by education policy writer and cognitive scientist Benjamin Riley took a look under the hood of Alpha School, a private K-12 school company in the US which boasts an AI-based teaching model, among other highly dubious features. To make the grade, high school students are required to pass an eight-week “bootcamp.” After being invited to follow social media accounts of other students at Alpha School, Riley observed a process more akin to hazing than high school orientation, as the private company made its prospective pupils jump through increasingly degrading hoops to secure their spot on the roster. For example, students in the bootcamp are required to start a brand-new account on X-formerly-Twitter, for which they’re required to get a minimum of 100 organic followers, pay for X Premium, and post an unknown number of replies to “bigger accounts with real substance,” as one student explained. “‘Great post’ replies don’t count,” the anonymous student posted in a screenshot shared by Riley. “Buying followers or spamming = automatic fail.” But forced social media assignments are just the beginning. As part of the bootcamp, students are also required to pass an eyebrow-raising heart rate challenge, in which they’re hooked up to live monitors in front of their classmates and presented with a pre-recorded video of their parents berating them. “The challenge rule was simple,” one student wrote: “my resting heart rate was 80bpm, and I had to stay within 10 percent of that the whole time. Anything above 88 was a fail.” It isn’t clear what educational purpose this particular trial was supposed to serve, or what the penalty would be for students who fail the heart rate challenge, or the boot camp overall. At least one student said the eight-week hazing period is meant to “prove I deserve to be here,” though Alpha School didn’t comment on Riley’s inquiry. “I ended up averaging 87bpm,” the student continued, evidently just barely making the grade. “Barely made the cutoff by one single beat, and my hands were shaking the entire rest of the afternoon.” As part of boot camp, Alpha School students apparently have their heart rates monitored as they watch recorded videos of their parents critiquing them. This activity takes place in front of their classmates, and students must keep their heart rates within 10 percent of their baseline to pass. Additionally, Riley noted that Alpha School “requires students to do unpaid labor cleaning restaurant kitchens or gas-station bathrooms” in a test of “grit and hard work.” This “assignment,” confirmed by two Alpha School pupils, involved students recording themselves and narrating their tasks, with any complaint or negative comment resulting in an automatic fail. “We have to find and execute an unglamorous job with zero pay, whether that means mucking horse stalls, cleaning grease traps after closing, washing trash bins, or scrubbing gas station bathrooms,” one of the students explained. Other challenges abound, like a requirement to complete an entire unit of a standard Advanced Placement course without a teacher — AI chatbots only — and pass an exam within just five days. One 14-year-old student wrote that they were required to survive for three days in an unfamiliar section of wilderness “with only 3 bags of rice and a tent.” Contacted by Riley for comment, Alpha School flipped the script, hounding the writer for daring to report on the “social media activity of minor children.” “We ask that you weigh the ethics of identifying minors in this way in your reporting, and we expect any published piece to protect their identities,” an Alpha School representative said. Responding to Riley’s story, educator and writer Audrey Watters said that “Alpha School’s promises have always been mostly snake-oil: this idea that artificial intelligence can deliver high-speed, high-quality education is mostly the stuff of science fiction, not reality.” “What we witness with Alpha School isn’t a matter of simply ignoring the weatherman, fingers-crossed hoping that the sun will continue to shine on the chosen few and that the winds will shift again in industry’s favor,” Watters continued, referring to rising outrage against AI, and the Silicon Valley-ethos underlying Alpha School’s mission. “Rather, it’s pretty clear that there’s an absence of any moral compass at all.” More on AI in education: The Results of Middle and High School Students Using AI Are Extremely Ominous

Elon Musk Seems Legitimately Shaken Over This New Documentary

Futurism | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. It’s no secret that Elon Musk is hiding plenty of skeletons in his closet. The mercurial billionaire has made an extensive list of enemies through his offensive commentary, led the gutting of humanitarian aid, exploited workers at his companies, and meddled with the democratic process — all the while alienating investors with his chronic overpromising and underdelivering. Simply put, documentarian and investigative journalist Alex Gibney had a very rich source of material to draw from for his upcoming documentary “Musk,” which had its world premiere today at the ongoing Venice Film Festival. According to journalist David Sirota, Musk is “freaking out” about the film because Gibney “doesn’t pull punches, doesn’t cower and won’t be bullied.” “And so the bully is mad,” Sirota tweeted. By all accounts, the four-hour epic will be a doozy. The documentarian “crafted a movie that, while not new in its specifics, is often damning in its totality, standing apart from anything the media entertainment complex has recently turned out — a portrait of power with the cinematic scope of ‘All the King’s Men’ and the potential social impact of ‘The Pentagon Papers,'” the Hollywood Reporter gushed. Musk has reportedly gone to extreme — and sometimes unsuccessful — lengths to silence those involved in Gibney’s latest work. Musk’s ex and former conservative influencer Ashley St. Clair, who is “prominently featured” in the documentary, told reporters at the Venice Film Festival that she was “offered the opportunity not to speak for $40 million,” as quoted by People. “When you’re offered something like that [to stay quiet], it really causes a debate in your head about what your speech means and how valuable that is and why do they want to offer me this amount of money to not speak?” St. Clair said. St. Clair is one of four women who have had a total of 14 children with Musk, at least that we know about so far. The 26-year-old had a major falling out with the entrepreneur, going as far as to allege that the billionaire “financially retaliated” against her for filing a custody petition by reducing child support payments. We’re likely to hear more commentary from Musk about the film. Last week, Musk tweeted that Gibney was “obviously going to make the most convincingly terrible hit piece on me that he can possibly think of,” calling him a “douchebag to his core.” But considering the staggering amount of material Musk has gifted the world, Gibney had plenty of material to work with. Futurism
Fashion Founder Emily Oberg Says She Tried Using AI for Photo Shoots, and It Was a Failure Because It Will “Never Possess Good Taste” Like Humans Do
2026-09-08

Fashion Founder Emily Oberg Says She Tried Using AI for Photo Shoots, and It Was a Failure Because It Will “Never Possess Good Taste” Like Humans Do

Futurism | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Can AI have good taste? Fashion founder and tastemaker Emily Oberg says no. In a post on her Substack, Emily Loves, Oberg discussed her experiments using AI to help her run Sporty & Rich, the fashion and lifestyle brand she founded in 2018 on the back of a popular moodboard she created. One area where she’s been testing AI, she wrote, is by using AI to generate e-commerce images — and according to the founder’s postmortem, the test was a failure. “With the rise of AI and the ongoing discourse around the threat it has on creativity, jobs and society as a whole, I firmly believe that it will never possess good taste the way that a human does,” Oberg declared. “And this is why I don’t think it’s as much of a threat to creatives as people think.” E-commerce imagery, Oberg said, is one of her brand’s steepest expenses, costing “upwards of” $200,000 for each twice-yearly shoot. Wanting to see if AI could help her get around these significant costs, Sporty & Rich used AI to generate e-commerce images to model its last collection. While the imagery may have been far cheaper than photos modeled, photographed, and edited by humans, however, the cost-cutting came with a catch: it just wasn’t very good. “This was one area I thought we could try to save on but after having seen the result, I’m now going back to producing our [e-commerce] with a full team of humans, because AI could never achieve the results that my IRL team could,” Oberg reflected, adding that her brand’s current images have a “certain lifelessness to them.” “They’re flat, the styling is mediocre and the images are too ‘perfect’ in a bad way,” Oberg continued. “The models look dead in the eyes and don’t have any organic movement whatsoever. The images are empty and void of any feeling or emotion.” Oberg chalked up the AI’s failure to the reality that taste is “felt, not learned.” And image-generating AI models are, after all, learning models; they don’t have lived experiences that translate into a particular way of viewing the world around them and the objects within it, nor do they have a certain “je ne sais quoi.” Though taste is a way through which people communicate with each other, taste itself is something that’s often unexplainable. “The smartest AI in the world can learn everything there is to know about taste and style,” she added, “but the feeling will never be there.” It’s interesting to see this AI experiment fail at Sporty & Rich, a brand that, like peers including Glossier and Goop, is a definitional of a certain type of deeply aesthetics-driven venture pioneered on social media platforms like Pinterest, Tumblr, and Instagram during the 2010s. Oberg was able to capture a feeling, first translated through a collection of carefully-curated imagery from across the web, and bottle it into a successful line of fashion and lifestyle products designed to embody a particular “health is wealth” ethos. That today’s AI models apparently can’t adequately capture that same feeling or aesthetic in a way that feels satisfying, or even human, speaks volumes to its remaining limits in the creative world. People “who truly have good taste don’t follow trends, they set them,” wrote Oberg. “More than this, they set them without trying to, it happens by just being themselves and liking what they like, not because an algorithm or AI told them to.” A key question that remains is whether other brands will continue to view a human touch worth it for the cost — or if they’ll charge ahead with AI nonetheless. More on AI and creativity: AI “Filmmaker” Gets Funding, Begs For Ideas On What to Actually Make

With this launch, Shipt joins a growing list of delivery platforms baking AI assistants into their apps. Just this morning, Instacart launched a new AI-powered grocery shopping assistant called Clementine, while Uber Eats and DoorDash have also introduced similar AI assistants this year. Additionally, users can ask for ideas based on their budget, such as a weeknight meal for a family of five under $35. The tool’s launch follows the recent addition of other AI features on Target.com itself, including AI-powered photo search, customer review summaries, and other personalized shopping experiences. Uber invests $10M in Indian fleet operator Carrum at $168M valuation
2026-09-09

Uber invests $10M in Indian fleet operator Carrum at $168M valuation

TechCrunch | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Uber has invested $10 million in Indian fleet management startup Carrum Mobility in a Series B round as the ride-hailing giant increases its reliance on large fleet operators to supply vehicles and drivers in the South Asian nation. The new investment values Carrum at ₹16 billion (about $168 million) post-money, founder and CEO Karan Jain told TechCrunch, up from a post-money valuation of ₹6 billion (around $63 million) after Uber invested $7 million in the firm in January. Jain said Uber now owns a stake in the “mid-teens” in Carrum. A former McKinsey consultant who previously founded car-rental startup Revv, Jain started Carrum in 2024 after Indian automotive marketplace CarDekho acquired his earlier company in 2023. CarDekho was also Carrum’s first investor and remains a backer. Carrum now owns about 5,100 vehicles across Bengaluru, Hyderabad, Mumbai, Pune, Delhi and Kolkata, and has onboarded more than 18,000 drivers. The startup is currently generating annualized revenue of about ₹4.3 billion (around $45 million), Jain said. The startup supplies vehicles to Uber in India for its entry-level Uber Go, Premier, and the premium Black tiers. About 70% of its fleet are hatchbacks used for Uber Go, around 10% are sedans for the Premier tier, and about 20% are SUVs, largely deployed on Uber Black. Carrum is Uber’s largest fleet partner for Black in India, Jain added. Unlike individual drivers who typically own or finance their vehicles, operators such as Carrum can put thousands of cars on Uber while recruiting and training drivers. The business model may be growing important for premium offerings. Jain said Uber Black in India operates exclusively through fleet partners, as the service requires tighter control over vehicles, drivers, and service standards. He also said Uber’s preference for fleet operators has become part of its supply strategy in other markets. Uber’s relationship with Carrum goes beyond a typical commercial arrangement, Jain said. The two companies are working on new product launches and planning how much vehicle supply to add to the ride-hail giant’s platform. Carrum is not exclusive to Uber, but Jain said his startup currently has no intention of supplying vehicles to rival ride-hailing platforms. The startup generated revenue of about ₹2.33 billion (around $24.5 million) in the year ended March 2026, up from around ₹620 million (about $6.5 million) a year ago, and net profit rose to about ₹70 million (around $736,000) from ₹35 million (about $368,000), Jain said. Carrum typically finances its vehicles with debt while funding about 10% to 15% of their purchase price upfront, Jain said. The firm’s borrowing costs, he stated, have fallen about 40% over the past year, which he attributed to its stronger balance sheet, profitability and Uber’s backing. Over the next 12 months, Carrum plans to more than double its fleet to about 11,000 vehicles, Jain said. The startup also plans to use the new capital to expand into more cities, strengthen its technology platform, and hire as it scales. Ultimately, Carrum’s ambitions extend beyond India, Jain said, noting that the startup wants to eventually become a global fleet partner for Uber. He declined to say whether the two companies have specifically discussed expanding their partnership outside India. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. AI spend per employee slumped at top firms in August — summer doldrums or a warning sign?
2026-09-09

AI spend per employee slumped at top firms in August — summer doldrums or a warning sign?

TechCrunch | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt The adoption of AI tools by businesses slowed in August, according to spending data at 70,000 companies collected by the payments company Ramp. The latest survey shows 56% of Ramp customers paid for AI products in August, rising just 0.4% from the month before. This isn’t the first time Ramp’s metrics have shown adoption slowing down. Last year, the company’s AI index showed little to no growth in adoption between August and October, only to have growth pick up again as the year finished. Still, the extreme pace of the AI buildout means even small slowdowns can be cause for concern. The gobsmacking investment in AI infrastructure by frontier labs and hyperscalers rests on the hope that there is plenty of revenue out there to pay it back. Thus far, usage has grown steeply, particularly as software engineers adopted agentic coding tools — but if that adoption slows down, revenue is likely to slow as well. Ramp’s figures may overstate overall adoption, thanks to the company’s techy clientele: An ongoing US Census Bureau survey of AI adoption updated on August 23 shows just 22% of businesses report using AI. Ramp’s survey isn’t necessarily representative of the market, but it’s one of the few direct spending data sets available and potentially a leading indicator. To be sure, this data is from August, when much of the industry is on vacation. That may explain the doldrums. But there are other warning signs for companies that depend on token spend, per Ramp economist Ara Kharazian. First, a major decline in AI spend per employee in the top 1% of firms in his sample, falling nearly 10% to $7,205. That may be the vacation-token factor, but it also speaks to falling token costs. As OpenAI and Anthropic have cut prices, average token costs have declined to $0.68 per million tokens, as opposed to the 2026 peak of $1.15 per million tokens in March. The data suggests that the labs have yet to make up for the price cuts with growing volume. And the same incentives have many customers choosing to use older, cheaper models like OpenAI’s ChatGPT 5.6-Terra and Anthropic’s Sonnet instead of the more powerful frontier releases. Employees at frontier labs have said much of the cost of training is recouped in the first weeks of a new model’s release, and slower adoption could threaten that dynamic. Still, for all the talk of open-weight models threatening the frontier labs, only 6.4% of AI-spending businesses used model-serving or inference platforms in August; a share that’s growing steadily but not fast enough to drive the dynamics of broader business adoption. “We are showing that competition between OpenAI and Anthropic is making AI more accessible, and also driving the price down for companies—and not just driving the price down, but driving spend down at the top 1% of companies that previously the market was expecting to drive much of the growth going forward,” Kharazian said. That also helps explain the focus at AI labs on winning over non-technical users for AI co-working tools. This data point — dare we call it a blip? — could be a bad sign if you’re a model-builder or a hyperscaler with a couple hundred billion of chips on order. But, Kharazian notes, “it depends on who you are in the market. If your company is using AI, it’s great.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Group of bipartisan lawmakers ask US government to ban several hack-for-hire firms
2026-09-09

Group of bipartisan lawmakers ask US government to ban several hack-for-hire firms

TechCrunch | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt A bipartisan group of U.S. lawmakers has asked the U.S. government to ban several hack-for-hire firms — companies that carry out cyberattacks on behalf of paying clients. The lawmakers accuse the firms of targeting Americans and abusing foreign courts to silence U.S. reporting on their activities. Democratic senators Ron Wyden of Oregon and Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island, and Republican congressman Pat Harrigan, on Wednesday sent a letter to U.S. Secretary of Commerce Howard Lutnick, urging him to add three Indian companies to the department’s economic sanctions “entity list.” The list effectively bars U.S. businesses from transacting with a named entity, with the aim of restricting the companies from accessing critical technology needed to function, including software licenses and cloud infrastructure. In the letter sent to Lutnick and shared with TechCrunch, the lawmakers say BellTroX, CyberRoot, and Sunkissed Organic Farms (which previously operated under its former name, Appin) have for more than a decade conducted cyberattacks and targeted espionage against Americans, business owners, and their lawyers, to “manipulate ongoing litigation.” The lawmakers say that these mercenary hacking companies have stolen data from thousands of Americans, and accuse the hackers of an “aggressive censorship campaign” to suppress public awareness of their alleged activities. “This coordinated effort effectively allows foreign entities to use foreign courts to keep the American public in the dark about cyber threats to their own country and undermines the fundamental constitutional rights of U.S. citizens,” the lawmakers said. It’s not clear if the Commerce Department will add the companies to its entity list, and a spokesperson did not respond to TechCrunch’s request for comment. The letter’s request to add the companies to the entity list follows extensive reporting and media investigations on the hack-for-hire industry, documenting how hackers are paid to break into the inboxes and devices of executives, lawmakers, and military officials to gain an advantage in lawsuits or influence their outcomes. One of the companies, Appin, previously secured a global court order from an Indian court forcing Reuters to take down its reporting on the company while Reuters appealed the order. The notice that appeared on the page at the time said that Reuters “stands by its reporting.” The order was later lifted and the report republished. The digital rights group Electronic Frontier Foundation previously defended two news organizations, Techdirt and the MuckRock Foundation, from legal threats after Appin engaged in “a campaign of bullying and censorship seeking to wipe out stories” about the company’s involvement in mercenary hacking. The lawmakers’ letter said that the hack-for-hire companies “operated at the behest of the Qatari government” and that their targets included a former senior Republican lawmaker. Appin has been linked to Qatar before. Earlier reporting tied Appin to a campaign of cyberattacks against FIFA officials, reportedly directed by Qatar as part of an effort to protect its plans to host the 2022 World Cup. A representative from the Qatari government in Washington, D.C. did not respond to TechCrunch’s request for comment. An email from TechCrunch to Anuj Khare, a director at Sunkissed Organic Farms, went unreturned. Separate reporting by The New Yorker and digital investigative unit The Citizen Lab has also documented espionage activity by the two other hack-for-hire firms named in the lawmakers’ letter, BellTroX and CyberRoot. TechCrunch sought comment from representatives at CyberRoot, but did not hear back prior to publication. BellTroX could not be reached for comment. Instacart launches an AI grocery shopping assistant called Clementine
2026-09-09

Instacart launches an AI grocery shopping assistant called Clementine

TechCrunch | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Instacart is the latest app to bake a conversational AI assistant into its platform. The company announced on Wednesday that it’s launching a new AI-powered grocery shopping assistant called Clementine that can turn a conversation, grocery list, or recipe into a ready-to-buy cart. With Clementine, users can decide what to make for dinner, simplify grocery shopping, and stay on budget, the company says. For example, users can ask the assistant queries like, “I need a week of budget-friendly kids’ lunches,” “high-protein, easy dinners for two,” or “order my usuals.” It’s worth noting that Instacart isn’t the only delivery app using AI to simplify grocery shopping for users, as Uber Eats and DoorDash have both introduced similar AI assistants that can turn natural-language requests and grocery lists into personalized carts. The launch of the AI assistants indicates that delivery apps no longer just want to be places where people find and buy groceries, but also where they make decisions before a purchase. These platforms are now competing not only on delivery speed and variety, but also on becoming the default planning tool for households when it comes to grocery shopping. Additionally, they’re looking to keep users in their own apps for grocery and meal planning, rather than having them turn to popular AI chatbots like ChatGPT. Instacart says Clementine can generate personalized recipes or surface ones from trusted publishers. It also tailors recommendations to each household’s preferences, including dietary needs such as gluten-free, vegetarian, nut-free, or organic options. The AI assistant will also surface relevant deals and promotions while suggesting lower-cost alternatives. Additionally, users can upload a photo of a handwritten grocery list or a screenshot of a digital one, and Clementine can turn it into a cart. “Every night, millions of families ask the same question: ‘What’s for dinner?’ Not knowing the answer, let alone what to shop for, is one of the most universal human experiences, and a problem Instacart is built to solve,” said Chris Rogers, CEO of Instacart, in an emailed press release. “We’ve spent nearly 15 years learning how families shop and eat, and Clementine puts that knowledge to work.” Clementine is now available in the U.S. and Canada. TechCrunch
Autonomy pivots to gas vehicles to keep the dream of car subscriptions alive
2026-09-09

Autonomy pivots to gas vehicles to keep the dream of car subscriptions alive

TechCrunch | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Four years ago, California startup Autonomy pledged to buy 23,000 EVs from 17 automakers, including Tesla, and make them available as part of a vehicle subscription service. It was a combination of two trends that had taken off in the early 2020s. Within a year, Autonomy was almost out of business, largely due to an EV price war started by Elon Musk, who was trying to keep Tesla competitive against a rush of new electric vehicles. Autonomy’s fleet, which had barely grown past 1,000 vehicles, lost around a third of its value. Founder Scott Painter (who also created TrueCar) had to more or less bail out the company while major automakers abandoned their own plans for vehicle subscriptions. The startup has stayed alive, however, and it hasn’t given up on vehicle subscriptions. Instead, Autonomy said on Wednesday that it is adding internal combustion engine (ICE) vehicles to its fleet for the first time, and is betting the pivot towards a more familiar powertrain will bring in customers. “If you’re going to be successful in anything, you’ve got to give the customer what the customer wants,” Autonomy’s CEO, Fred Weick, told TechCrunch in an exclusive interview. “There’s very few examples, I think, in history, of creating things customers didn’t know they wanted.” The new lineup will feature gas-powered Ford vehicles like the Mustang, Ranger and F-150 pickups, as well as SUVs like the Bronco Sport, Escape and Explorer. Autonomy is sourcing the vehicles from Los Angeles-based Galpin Motors and making them available to customers in California. The company also operates in Arizona, Florida, Texas, New York, North Carolina and Washington, and said it will work with other dealer partners in these markets. Autonomy is renewing its bet on vehicle subscriptions at a time when new car prices soar above $50,000. Used cars are getting more expensive, too. Weick, who spent more than 20 years at Mercedes-Benz, said rising prices are making it harder for people with low credit scores — or no access to credit at all — to get a vehicle. His company charges a one-time fee (currently $1,000 for the EVs) and then a set price per month, which varies depending on the make and model. After one month, customers can cancel the subscription at any time. Weick said Autonomy is targeting four types of customers with this push into ICE vehicles: university students, military families, foreign workers, and people who want a “company car” experience. “The crux of the interest is easy and quick access to mobility without all the headaches that come with the old school” way of buying cars, he said. “The past [business] models were all about trying to fit a new concept into old shoes, and that doesn’t work.” Weick said Autonomy is still seeing interest for EVs, especially in California. The company currently maintains a fleet of a little more than 500 electric cars, which is far from the 23,000 it had promised in 2022. But it’s hardly the only fleet company to have fumbled the transition. Hertz claimed in 2021 that it was going to buy as many as 100,000 Teslas (and more from other automakers), but wound up selling a majority of them in 2024 in favor of gas vehicles. It's Head Lice Season. Here's What to Know

Update your preferences in Account Settings As children return to school, families have a lot to look forward to: fresh routines, new connections, crisper weather—and a greater risk of head lice. September is National Head Lice Prevention Month in the U.S, coinciding with a time of year when infestations tend to surge. “Head lice can occur throughout the year, but cases are often noticed more frequently in late summer and early fall, when children return to school and resume close-contact activities,” says Dr. April Armstrong, chief of dermatology at the University of California, Los Angeles. Head lice is a common childhood condition, with up to 12 million head lice infestations occurring among children ages 3 to 11 every year in the U.S., according to estimates from the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). The insects don’t spread disease, but they can still cause a great deal of distress, doctors say. “Having head lice is not a sign of poor hygiene, but it can cause stress and anxiety because there is this stigma that it is,” says Dr. Carla Torres Zegarra, a pediatric dermatologist at Children’s Hospital Colorado. “If left untreated, it can also be very bothersome, causing itching and disturbing sleep.” Here’s what to know about the pesky parasites, and how to spot, treat, and prevent them. Head lice, or Pediculus humanus capitis, are tiny, wingless insects that feed on human blood. They are found mostly on the scalp, but sometimes appear on eyebrows and eyelashes. Adult head lice are about the size of a sesame seed and are usually tan to grayish-white in color, according to the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP). Head lice lay and attach their eggs—known as nits—to the base of hair shafts using a glue-like substance. Infested people may have a combination of adult and juvenile head lice, nits, and empty egg casings on their scalp and in their hair. No. There are three different types of lice that feed exclusively on humans: head lice, pubic or “crab” lice, and body lice. Unlike head lice, which have elongated bodies, pubic lice have short and round bodies, resembling crabs. Pubic lice typically spread through sexual contact and are most common in adults. When people have body lice, the insects are found primarily on their clothing and bedding, and sometimes on their bodies. Unlike head lice, body lice is connected to hygiene practices and access to clean clothing, and spread primarily through direct contact with someone who has body lice or with their contaminated clothing or bedding. In the U.S., body lice mostly affects people without access to housing, clean clothes, and regular bathing, CDC says. Since head lice can only crawl and not jump or fly, they typically spread via head-to-head contact. “That’s why it happens more in kids, because they play in close proximity,” Torres Zegarra says. Head lice can also spread through objects that touch the head, such as combs, hats, helmets, and pillowcases. But this type of indirect transmission is much less likely since head lice typically survive for less than a day when away from the scalp, and nits can’t hatch at temperatures cooler than that of the human scalp, AAP says. Getting head lice has nothing to do with personal hygiene or the cleanliness of the environment, and is caused solely by exposure to an infested person. If there’s an outbreak of lice at your child’s school or daycare, that’s not a guarantee they’ll get it. Though head lice frequently spread in schools, research has found that head lice actually have a low contagion rate in classrooms. That’s why the CDC, AAP, National Association of School Nurses and other medical groups don’t recommend keeping children with head lice away from school. An itchy scalp is the most common symptom of head lice, “particularly behind the ears and near the back of the neck,” Armstrong says. “Some people also describe a tickling sensation or the feeling that something is moving in the hair. Scratching may produce redness, small sores, or, occasionally, a secondary bacterial infection.” People can have head lice without symptoms, too. “With a first infestation, itching may not begin for four to six weeks because it takes time for the body to develop an allergic response to louse saliva,” Armstrong says. Examining the hair and scalp under bright light and finding live crawling lice is the best way to diagnose a head lice infestation. Using a magnifying glass or a special lice comb can help with this process, AAP says. “Nits located farther from the scalp are often empty or no longer viable, so their presence alone does not necessarily indicate an active infestation,” Armstrong says. Misdiagnosis of head lice is common. “Many presumed lice and nits submitted by physicians, nurses, teachers, and caregivers to a laboratory for identification were found to be artifacts such as dandruff, hairspray droplets, scabs, dirt, or other insects,” such as those blown by the wind and caught in the hair, AAP says. The CDC recommends that people visit a doctor if they suspect they may have lice. Head lice infestations most commonly occur in preschool- and elementary school-age children and their families. Research suggests that girls may get head lice more often than boys. But the AAP says head lice infestations do not appear to be influenced by hair length, hair type, and frequency of shampooing or brushing. There are several over-the-counter and prescription medications approved by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to treat head lice, including lotions and shampoos that contain the insecticides permethrin or pyrethrin. Treatment of head lice often involves using one of these medications, combined with “careful combing using a fine-toothed lice comb,” Armstrong says. Some of these medications kill only lice, while others target both lice and nits. Depending on the medication, a second application may be needed seven to ten days after the first treatment, Armstrong says. In some cases, head lice can be resistant to these topical medications, AAP says. In those situations, manual removal of lice and nits might be necessary—an option also available to people who want to avoid insecticide-containing products. The AAP says home remedies such as using mayonnaise, melted butter, or olive oil could work to suffocate the lice, though these methods are not FDA approved or recommended, and more research still needs to be done on their effectiveness. A prescription oral medication can also be used. Some internet sites recommend using essential oils and other botanical products to kill head lice, but the AAP says “the safety and efficacy of natural or herbal products are currently not regulated by the FDA, and until more data are available, their use in infants, children, and adolescents should be avoided.” The AAP also stresses that head lice treatment is only recommended for people “in whom diagnosis is highly suspected or proven.” Torres Zegarra says she sometimes sees patients with itchy scalps who developed irritation because they tried head lice treatments despite not having any evidence of lice. “What I end up treating is not lice but the skin irritation caused by multiple treatments that were tried on the scalp,” she says. Doctors say that cutting or shaving the hair of an infested person isn’t necessary, and it does not always work to get rid of head lice. “Long or very thick hair may require more product and more time to comb thoroughly, but it can be treated successfully without removing the hair,” Armstrong says. “If live lice remain after treatment has been used correctly, a clinician should determine whether the cause is medication resistance, reinfestation, or an incorrect diagnosis before additional treatments are applied.” “There is no completely reliable way to prevent head lice, especially among young children who naturally play in close contact. The most effective step is to limit direct head-to-head or hair-to-hair contact when lice are known to be circulating,” Armstrong says. If someone is confirmed to have head lice, members of their household and other close contacts should be carefully checked for infestation. “Clothing, towels, and bedding used during the two days before treatment can be washed and dried using heat,” Armstrong says. “Combs and brushes can be soaked in hot water. Extensive cleaning, fumigation, and pesticide sprays are unnecessary.” AAP says parents and other caregivers could check children regularly to catch and treat early infestations, and children can be taught not to share personal items such as combs and hats. They shouldn’t refuse to wear helmets because of fear of head lice, however, particularly since the spread of lice through objects is uncommon, AAP says. Ultimately, Torres Zegarra says “there’s no need to panic” when head lice arise. “It’s a nuisance problem but not a health crisis,” she says, adding that children with head lice shouldn’t be quarantined or kept away from school. “No healthy child should lose out on education because of head lice. They should avoid head-to-head contact, but they shouldn’t be shamed or isolated,” Torres Zegarra says.

12 Questions You’ll Be Glad You Asked Your Grandparents
2026-09-09

12 Questions You’ll Be Glad You Asked Your Grandparents

TIME | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Elizabeth Keating’s students learn about World War II in history books. But when one of them asked her grandmother what it was like to live through the wartime mobilization and bombing in the U.K., the past suddenly felt real. Talking to her grandmother, the student told Keating, felt like “talking to a celebrity.” “It gives us a link with history that’s very personal,” says Keating, a professor emerita of anthropology at the University of Texas at Austin and author of The Essential Questions: Interview Your Family to Uncover Stories and Bridge Generations. Your grandparents might not think of themselves as eyewitnesses to history—or realize that anyone is interested in the ordinary details of their lives. Asking an unexpected question can make the past come alive while helping you discover who they were, and who they still are. These questions can help you get beyond the familiar family stories—and start conversations you’ll be glad you had. Your grandparent has probably changed their mind about more than a few things over the years. Maybe they swore they would never get married or that they’d stay in their hometown forever. Ask what they felt certain about at your age—and what eventually made them reconsider. The answer can make a younger person think, “Oh my God, you were my age,” says Mike O’Krent, founder of LifeStories Alive, a company that preserves family histories on video. It might also be reassuring to discover that your grandparent once shared some of your fears or wrestled with questions you’re confronting right now. Hearing what happened next can open up a larger conversation about how they became the person they are today. “What was your childhood like?” is an enormous question—the conversational equivalent of handing someone a blank book and asking them to fill it. It’s better to zoom in on one specific setting. “The dinner table is a window into an entire family, sometimes an entire childhood,” says Lauren Befus, founder of Memory Lane Jane, a family-biography company that preserves people’s life stories in heirloom books. Ask who sat where, who cooked, and whether everyone ate together. Was dinner loud and chaotic or silent and formal? Did people linger or bolt the moment they finished? Was there always enough food? Could friends drop in? One person told Keating that “there was no talking at the dinner table.” In Keating’s own family, her mother occasionally made the children prepare little reports from the encyclopedia to present during dinner. “We hated it,” she recalls. Whether your grandparent remembers laughter, pinching under the table, or strictly enforced silence, you’ll learn much more than what was on the menu. Every family has at least one expenditure it considers financially unhinged. Perhaps your grandparent’s parents refused to pay someone to repair anything they could conceivably fix themselves. Maybe restaurant meals, store-bought clothes, or long-distance phone calls were treated as scandalous extravagances. The question can reveal what money represented in your grandparent’s childhood home: security, freedom, status, or a source of constant stress. You might also recognize a few financial commandments that have traveled down the family line. Oh, that’s why your mom refuses to pay for shipping or regards full-price cereal as a personal affront! Your grandparent wasn’t always the person reminding everyone to be careful. Once upon a time, they might have sneaked somewhere forbidden, played hooky from Sunday school to take a nap in the cornfields behind the church, concocted an impressive lie, or attempted to conceal a mishap from their parents. Keating particularly likes asking about trouble because it comes with a built-in plot: What happened? How did you respond? Did anyone find out? The story doesn’t need to involve a wild act of rebellion. She’s found that “you can get into trouble with small things, too, that evolve into bigger things.” Either way, you’ll get to meet the mischievous younger person behind the grandparent you know and love. That battered book, recipe card, or piece of jewelry might look unremarkable. Then you hear the story about why it’s still a prized possession. Keating recalls talking to a woman who had grown up poor and dreamed of owning a particular doll. When she received it for her birthday, she treasured it so deeply that she eventually passed it down through her family. “We attach relationships to objects,” Keating says. Eventually, “an object isn’t just a thing anymore.” It evokes a person, place, yearning, or moment someone wants to keep close. If your grandparent still has the item, ask to see it. Their act of holding it again—or simply studying its scratches, faded colors, or fraying edges—might shake loose a story they didn’t even realize they remembered. The answer could introduce you to a teacher, mentor, neighbor, boss, or friend you’ve never heard about—yet whose encouragement altered the course of your family’s history. For O’Krent, it was his third-grade teacher, Mrs. Glaze. One day, despite making him stay inside during recess because he couldn’t keep his mouth shut, she looked him in the eye and said: “You’re going to be somebody important someday.” He never forgot it. An influential person isn’t always who you would expect, O’Krent says, and the best part of the story may emerge when you ask why your grandparent connected with them. What did they see in your grandparent—and how did their belief change what your grandparent saw in themselves? Maybe your grandparent pursued a career their family didn’t approve of, moved somewhere unexpected, ended—or began—a relationship, or took a risk everyone else considered foolish. Ask why people objected. Then borrow O’Krent’s favorite follow-up: “Do you agree with that?” Your grandparent might still insist everyone else was wrong. Or perhaps the decision led directly to something they’d love to forget. “Everybody makes mistakes,” O’Krent says. What matters is “the lesson you learn from it.” Either way, you’ll discover how your grandparent responded when their judgment collided with other people’s expectations—and whether they’d make the same choice again. Résumés are good for recording promotions and degrees. But they don’t capture the time someone took care of an aging parent, raised children through a miserable year, started over after a failure, repaired a relationship, or became the friend who always showed up. The accomplishments you associate with your grandparent might not be the ones that matter most to them. This question “gives people permission to define accomplishment for themselves,” Befus says. You may discover that the chapter of their life they value most is one you barely knew about. There is, apparently, no birthday at which a person wakes up with life completely sorted out. Grandparents still have evolving identities, relationships, hopes, and uncertainties, says Claudia Johnson, a licensed marriage and family therapist in Seattle. Asking what your grandparent is still figuring out communicates: “I don’t only want to know who you were. I’m interested in who you are now,” Johnson says. Maybe they’re learning how to enjoy retirement, trying to understand a changing friendship, or deciding what they want the next few years to look like. Answer the question yourself, too. That reciprocity transforms the interaction from an interview into a real conversation—and reminds both of you that nobody ages out of uncertainty. Not every meaningful question requires rummaging through the past. That’s especially important when you’re talking to someone with dementia, says Cristina Rodriguez, founder of Mind&Melody, a nonprofit that connects musicians with people living with dementia and other neurological conditions. Rodriguez’s grandmother was diagnosed with dementia a few years ago. As her memory changes, Rodriguez has realized that “connection can’t always depend on asking her to recall the past.” During one FaceTime call, when Rodriguez was thinking about having children, she asked her grandmother what she would name a baby girl today. Estefania, she replied, because it sounded strong and powerful. Rodriguez had known her grandmother her entire life—and knew she had raised six children—but had never thought to ask her anything like that. The exchange showed Rodriguez “how much possibility there is in questions that aren’t about remembering at all.” Ask what song should belong to the two of you, or what your perfect day together would look like. These types of questions let you be curious about who your grandparent is right now, including what they imagine and what makes them laugh. After putting your grandparent in the hot seat, turn the interview over to them. What have they always wondered about you? “The grandparent’s not expecting that one,” O’Krent says. That’s exactly why he likes it: The question gives your grandparent some control over where the conversation goes and makes the exchange feel mutual. Once the conversation is flowing, don’t cling too tightly to your prepared questions. O’Krent teaches people an unusual listening trick: “The next thing you say has to include at least one word from the last sentence they say.” Because you don’t know when that last sentence is coming, you have no choice but to pay close attention. It might feel awkward at first, he acknowledges, but it helps the other person feel heard. Plus, the best question is almost always the follow-up you didn’t plan. This question might expose the blank spaces in your grandparent’s own family history. Maybe they know their mother moved across the country alone but never learned what compelled her to go. Perhaps they inherited a house without ever asking why their grandparents chose it—or what brought their parents together in the first place. The answers often involve what Keating calls “huge identity questions”: Where did I come from? Who am I? Why did my family’s life unfold the way it did? Most people know the milestones, she says, but what they really want is “the story behind everything.” Listen closely. Your grandparent might be telling you not only what they regret leaving unanswered, but which parts of their own story they hope you won’t.

How TIME and Statista Determined the World's Best Companies of 2026

How TIME and Statista Determined the World's Best Companies of 2026

TIME | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The research project "World’s Best Companies 2026" is a comprehensive analysis conducted to identify the top performing companies around the world. The study is based on three primary dimensions: Employee Satisfaction, Revenue Growth and Sustainability Transparency (ESG). The first dimension, Employee Satisfaction, was investigated based on survey data from a large sample of over 200,000 employees from around the world. The evaluation encompassed direct recommendations of verified employees as well as indirect evaluations from industry peers. The second dimension, Revenue Growth, was assessed using data from Statista’s revenue database, which contains company growth data for the last three years. The companies had to meet certain criteria to be considered for the evaluation, including generating a revenue of at least $100 million in 2024 or 2025 depending on when the most recent data was published during the research phase. Additionally, the companies had to demonstrate positive revenue growth in the last three years. Both relative and absolute growth were considered in the evaluation. The third dimension, Sustainability Transparency, was evaluated based on ESG data among standardized KPIs from Statista’s ESG Database and targeted data research. To formulate a comprehensive ESG index, multiple Key Performance Indicators were collected. For the environmental evaluation, this included the 2024 carbon emissions intensity and reduction rate compared to 2022, as well as the Carbon Disclosure Project (CDP) score. The social dimension assessed the share of women on the board of directors and the existence of a human rights policy. The governance dimension evaluated whether a company had a Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) report adhering to the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI) guidelines and a compliance or anti-corruption guideline. Once the data was collected and evaluated, it was consolidated and weighted within a scoring model. The scores of all three dimensions were added on an equal percentage basis to form the final ranking score of a maximum of 100 points. The 1,000 companies with the highest score were awarded as World’s Best Companies 2026 by TIME and Statista.
