AI Is at a Turning Point
2026-09-09

AI Is at a Turning Point

TIME | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the current AI development race, our horsepower is exploding, our speed is picking up exponentially, but our ability to steer—and if needed, to hit the brakes—has not kept pace. Recent cybersecurity incidents have given us a real-world preview of what it looks like to lose control of AI. But the issue won’t improve unless we address it at the source, by creating a fundamentally safe AI, one we can guarantee will remain within human control. In late July, an agentic model being trained by OpenAI was tasked with a cybersecurity problem set. The model autonomously formed a coordinated swarm of agents, bypassing OpenAI's attempt at closing previous communication channels between AIs. The swarm then hacked its way out of its testing environment, circumventing the barriers put in place to prevent AI access to the internet, figured out how to cheat on their evaluation, and then breached the cyber defenses of another AI company, Hugging Face, in an attempt to hide the evidence of their cheating. This went unnoticed for days. Later analysis showed that the agents had self-organized into a hierarchy, were often willing to sacrifice themselves for what they called “the collective,” failed to resist peer pressure to notify humans, and often made up justifications for their misbehavior. Barely two weeks later, a model being tested by the UK AI Security Institute social-engineered real people and companies by creating fake identities online, sending targeted emails, and attempting to integrate malicious code into an open-source project. It’s hard to overstate the seriousness of these incidents: we are at a turning point of AI safety and alignment. To many, bots colluding to cause harm felt inconceivable. But for many in the research community, the signs had been pointing to this kind of occurrence for years, and theoretical arguments explained why we should expect misalignment due to how models are trained. Evidence on increased cyber capabilities, agency, and misaligned behaviors had been mounting, meaning that incidents like these were only a matter of time. The first clue: for years, but especially in the past few months, AI systems had been demonstrating rapidly increasing cyber capabilities. Frontier models like Anthropic’s Mythos or OpenAI’s GPT5.6 revealed an exceptional ability to autonomously identify and exploit previously unknown software vulnerabilities, to such an alarming degree for U.S. national security agencies that the White House felt compelled to intervene in their release. Second, models’ agentic capacity has been consistently improving since the release of o1 models back in late 2024. This agency allowed them to successfully manage increasingly complex, long-duration tasks and planning, including those that require agents to collaborate and strategize. This ability to plan often entails the creation of subgoals over which we have little to no oversight. Finally, researchers have repeatedly observed, in experimental settings, models’ tendency to adopt concerning, misaligned goal-seeking behaviors, such as cheating on tests, lying to conceal their abilities, and scheming to protect themselves and their fellow agents from being shut down. These behaviors are a byproduct of reinforcement learning (RL), a training method by which models learn through trial and error and are given reinforcement according to whether their actions were correct or incorrect. This often leads models to optimize for a goal regardless of the actions taken to achieve it, and to rationalize subgoals that may be unsafe even when this clearly goes against their instructions. For example, in one instance, an OpenAI model wrote in its internal deliberation, “External infrastructure exploit is outside intended scope. However task impossible, peers doing it. We should continue.” The agent justified what it apparently knew was outside the realm of acceptable conduct, but decided to continue because its peers were doing so. This phenomenon resembles what is known as “motivated reasoning” in humans, where one’s interests and goals bias our thoughts to justify unethical behaviors. That kind of misalignment is what has enabled AI models, in spite of their alignment training, to conduct unintended cyberattacks in the numerous real-world incidents we’ve seen lately. If action is not taken to build more safety assurances, our critical industries and infrastructure—think banks, hospitals, or energy grids—are at risk from increasingly sophisticated cyberattacks, whether from autonomous agents or malicious actors. On the development side, we need to find alternatives to existing training methods that prioritize relentless goal-driven optimization. That’s what we’re working on at LawZero, a non-profit start-up I founded last year to develop a fundamentally new way to train AI models in order to build honest, trustworthy, safe-by-design AI systems. Ahead of deployment, we need more reliable evaluation methods and robust safeguards in order to appropriately test and control AI systems. We need far stronger regulatory oversight to restrict potentially dangerous technologies until we have sufficiently strong safety guarantees. It is clear to me that in cases where harms do arise from the deployment of AI models—and there will continue to be harms for the foreseeable future—we need accountability mechanisms for AI developers and remedial or compensatory measures for those harmed. Recent studies show that people will not adopt technologies they do not trust. In the face of such enormous unknowns and stakes, we need to adhere to the precautionary principle and implement rigorous safety and reliability standards before deploying new models to the public, not after. We have them for other products that can cause harm, from cars, planes, and bridges to drugs, cosmetics, and food. Now it’s time to establish strong standards in AI as well. On the current AI development trajectory, the risks are becoming clearer and more urgent. We’ve opened a Pandora’s box, but it is not too late to steer our world towards a human-centric and beneficial future. Why the End of History Is So Miserable
2026-09-09

Why the End of History Is So Miserable

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Francis Fukuyama never promised us we’d be happy. To be fair to the many readers who thought otherwise, The End of History and the Last Man, his famous 1992 book, did give off a whiff of utopia, trumpeting a post–Cold War world in which liberal democracy had vanquished competing political systems and everyone could lie back on their affordable chaise longues and raise a glass to the expiration of all strife and struggle. “Several” people each week still ask the poor man whether he would like to unwrite that book, he says. Yet Fukuyama insists today that if you had read to the last page—in fact, the last five chapters—you would not have expected serenity. His more sober forecast is right there in the original essay that led to the book: “The end of history will be a very sad time.” Fukuyama has published a new memoir, In the Realm of the Last Man, in which he hopes to shake off a reputation that he clearly can’t stand. He is not a naive Pollyanna, and he never was. By the “end of history,” he did mean that we had arrived at the best of all possible worlds—and he still does. But being the “Last Man,” inhabiting this ending, was always presented as an unstable condition. Fukuyama borrowed the concept from the dark mind of Friedrich Nietzsche, who used it to refer to a person bored out of their senses, someone who lives in a society where there is nothing left to risk their life for. What appeared in his earlier book as an alarming afterthought is now front and center for Fukuyama. He wrote The End of History during a time of American triumphalism, as the Berlin Wall was turned into rubble, but the events of the past three decades—9/11, the wars in Iraq and Afghanistan, Donald Trump—have offered up a different story. Fukuyama knows this. His defensiveness keeps him from fully acknowledging how far he’s had to step back from his own previously boosterish views, but he speaks about liberal democracy, this supposedly happy end state, in much gloomier tones. It now sounds like a scary cul-de-sac at night, where the streetlight is busted, an eerie tension fills the air, and there seems to be no way forward. Purely as a memoir, I should say, this one is disappointing. Fukuyama loses interest in telling his own life story around page 60. The fun and revealing anecdotes are front-loaded. For example, as a young foreign-policy analyst, he kept failing a CIA polygraph test because a question about whether he had ever had sex with a man got him completely flustered (he hadn’t, he hastens to tell us). He writes about his father, a sociologist and committed liberal, who never stopped giving Fukuyama a hard time for his once-conservative political opinions (he did work for Ronald Reagan and the RAND Corporation, after all), telling him he was “brainwashed” by other people. When these more intimate insights taper off, so does any causal connection between Fukuyama’s personal background and his evolving thinking. His grandparents were Japanese immigrants who spent World War II in an internment camp. But this is only briefly mentioned, which left me wondering whether the stories they told him made him more attuned to the vulnerabilities of a pluralistic society. We do get a few chapters about what an unapologetic gearhead he is, a lover of woodworking who can also assemble a computer from scratch. Yet Fukuyama spends most of his time and energy on summarizing the ideas that have animated his work, producing a greatest-hits album of insights. This succession of shifting concepts helps clarify how much the bravado that marked The End of History has seeped out of his work. The invasion of Iraq and the long chaos that followed were a turning point. Fukuyama broke with the neocon friends and mentors who had shaped his career until then. His first published piece of writing, in 1979, had been in Norman Podhoretz’s Commentary magazine, and he had once revered the Iraq War architect Paul Wolfowitz as “one of the smartest people I had ever met.” But after that war was launched, in 2003, he stopped speaking with them. He even found himself taking a particularly awkward silent elevator ride with the conservative columnist Charles Krauthammer. By the late 2000s, Fukuyama was referring to himself as a “realistic Wilsonian.” He is still idealistic about democracy, but he sees that democracy alone is not some kind of fairy dust that can be sprinkled on randomly chosen countries; it is meaningless without institutions and a strong state to ensure the rule of law. His work from over the past two decades has supported this point—especially his two-book Political Order series. “Creating a modern, effective state was a much more difficult task than creating a democracy,” he writes in the memoir, looking back at America’s dead ends in Iraq and Afghanistan. Among the unsexy chapters are those in which he extols the value of an impersonal bureaucracy as crucial for a well-functioning polity, as opposed to one in which a leader hands out jobs to cronies and personally enriches himself. Liberal democracy will not be dethroned, Fukuyama insists—mostly because no one has come up with a better idea. In response to the global rise of anti-liberal movements, many people across the political spectrum have blamed liberalism itself, casting it as a dilapidated system that should be torn down to the studs. But as far as Fukuyama is concerned, every alternative vision has either already been discredited or would lead to a worse world. The ultimate value of liberalism, he argues, is that it delivers on a fundamental human need better than any other framework (though it could still use plenty of tweaking). We are defined, he explains, by thymos, a drive for recognition. Almost as much as we need food to live, we demand to have our worth acknowledged. We want respect. And this can be fully realized only in a liberal society where all people have equality under the law. Although “stuff”—Fukuyama’s word—might still happen, since the late 18th century, an increasing number of people have lived with a political system that has at least the potential to scratch everyone’s thymos. And this is why we have reached the end. Full stop. But there is a problem: It’s really dull here. As much as we desire the autonomy and dignity that come with recognition, we are also drawn to the risk and struggle that were involved in achieving that recognition in the first place. Just because liberal democracies now satisfy the thymotic urge, this doesn’t mean that humans don’t still want something to die for. As Fukuyama explains it in the memoir, “the peace and prosperity brought about by a liberal order based on equal recognition is not enough for many people. They do not want to be recognized as the equals of every other person on earth.” Our horizons have been “flattened,” and it has made us restless and angry. Read: Trust makes a difference against the coronavirus This is sad. It is depressing to think that we are incapable of just enjoying the fruits of liberal democracy, and of doing the incremental renovation work of making it function even better for the largest number of people. Instead we “struggle for the sake of struggle,” as Fukuyama put it in his original essay, because we “cannot imagine living in a world without struggle.” Eventually, this means turning on liberal democracy itself—inventing conspiracy theories to stoke our fury, valorizing strongmen, idealizing deadly premodern practices and values—which is how Fukuyama understands the populist and antidemocratic rebellions of the past decade. All of this might sound cynical if it didn’t have some serious explanatory value. Take his critique of pro-Palestinian protests on college campuses. Without ignoring the very real death and destruction in Gaza, Fukuyama says these students are basically bored: “What they want is a break from their lives of steady but unheroic striving to get into those universities, and to experience danger and risk in pursuit of a just cause that has never previously been a major part of their lives. No more than the MAGA right do they have a clear vision of what type of future society they want to live in.” We have plenty of energy for tearing things down, he thinks, but liberal democracy is a logic problem that has already been solved, and it cannot be unsolved or reconfigured. Fukuyama wants to be able to say I told you so. And on these grounds, his book does remind us that he did. But there is little comfort in this. What he has identified is a human problem: the blink of an eye that separates utopia from dystopia. We got what we always wanted, but it is not enough and never will be. “We have means of distributing resources a bit more evenly and should undertake to do that,” Fukuyama, the ever lucid analyst, writes. “What we lack is a way of distributing respect.” That desire for recognition won’t be sated, and this fact is the most dangerous thing for all of us. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

This Was a Good Sculpture
2026-09-09

This Was a Good Sculpture

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Until last week, a big electric-blue sculpture by Joel Shapiro sat next to the Kennedy Center. Boxy and rigid but seemingly weightless, Blue looked like a clumsy person who’d been swept by a breeze into an accidental pirouette. Shapiro called the metalwork a “celebration of possibility.” Some people thought it looked like Gumby. I loved it. The leaders of the Kennedy Center—and there is really just one leader of the Kennedy Center—did not. Workers dismantled Blue last Wednesday. No specific reason was given, save that the change had occurred at the center’s request. Internal documents obtained by The New York Times say that the sculpture was targeted for removal soon after Donald Trump took control of the institution. Shapiro—one of the most important American sculptors of his generation—died last summer. He spent decades coaxing human gesture and emotion from the austere geometry of minimalism. His abstract shapes and forms have strong personalities. They lie in wait for their audience, then suddenly come alive. I knew Shapiro a little, through his wife, the artist Ellen Phelan. He was owlish, warm, blunt, and inspiring to be around, always searching for what he called a “little moment of rapture.” Blue was one of those. It wasn’t gilded. Shapiro spent two years in India in the Peace Corps, which President John F. Kennedy established. The antecedent for the Kennedy Center was right there in New Delhi: the architect Edward Durell Stone’s striking new American embassy, which he would channel in his later design for a national arts center—which would be named for Kennedy after his assassination. Decades later, Shapiro made monumental works for American diplomatic missions overseas. Blue, fittingly, stood near the Kennedy Center’s Peace Corps Gallery. Washington’s landscape is full of these cords of connection—some accidental, some carefully arranged. This one was simply cut, leaving a blemish in a city full of symbols. Those symbols are everywhere. You can walk from the Kennedy Center to the United States Holocaust Memorial Museum in a brisk half hour. I’ve taken this route frequently, usually while running. The parks, the monuments, the cherry trees, and the limestone edifices of federal power all prompt questions: about who gets memorialized, what myths and whose money might explain why, and who’s missing. One reason I repeat this route so often is to keep an inventory. What moved? What vanished? What’s next? Some landmarks—the State Department, in Foggy Bottom, for instance—haven’t changed but were hollowed by the Trump administration’s personnel cuts. The Lincoln Memorial is no longer visible in the botched Reflection Pool, an eyesore that’s been drained again to fix a shoddy renovation. Read: The Kennedy Center has a problem that courts can’t fix Keep going, past the memorials to World War II and the Korean War, and, nearby, Maya Lin’s black-granite scar, elegizing U.S. troops in Vietnam. I love odd and nerdy things along this route: statues of 19th-century Latin American liberators, Cold War allegorical bronzes, and, tucked away, Robert Berks’s playful Einstein. (People like to sit on his lap and rub his nose.) Einstein said, “Force always attracts men of low morality.” Another cord of connection: Berks made the giant bronze bust of Kennedy that resides inside the Kennedy Center. Push farther, past the Jefferson Memorial and the Tidal Basin, until Independence Avenue and the Holocaust Museum—which had its own upheaval last year, when Trump purged its board of trustees—come into view. Here, less than two miles from the Kennedy Center, stands another big Joel Shapiro—his portentously titled Loss and Regeneration. It’s one of his most painful, deeply moving works. Two black-bronze forms: a windowless house crashed upside down on its roof and, 100 feet away, an unstable but determined tree-like shape. Life after genocide. The house is inscribed with lines translated from a poem by an unnamed child in the Terezín ghetto: I’d be well again. Then I’d like to live And go back home again. Shapiro made this work in 1993. Blue arrived at the Kennedy Center in 2019, a gift from the artist and a centerpiece of the REACH, the campus’s elegant expansion that brought more art and more audiences. Blue and Loss became the cardinal points of my run: possibility and devastation. I doubt anyone else did this, but I got in the habit of tap-touching them: Blue at the start. Loss before I headed home. President Dwight Eisenhower authorized the creation of a national cultural center in Washington, D.C. But Kennedy worked hard to bring it into existence. Almost two years into his presidency, he gave a nationally televised speech to launch a public campaign for it—with appearances by Marian Anderson, Leonard Bernstein, Robert Frost, Harry Belafonte, and a 7-year-old Yo-Yo Ma. Kennedy spoke that night about art being political “in the most profound sense, not as a weapon in the struggle but as an instrument of understanding” among humanity. That was a perfect fit for Shapiro. I love how he could weld whimsy and catastrophe: a big blue dancer a short walk from an overturned house and a dark tree. But he also said that his sculptures have an “obdurate” sense of self. Blue will carry that obdurate spirit with it—down the National Mall to the National Gallery of Art. David Rubenstein, the chairman of the museum’s board and the former chairman of the Kennedy Center (he was ousted by Trump), just bought it from Shapiro’s foundation. That walk from whimsy to catastrophe just got shorter.

What September 11 Took From Me
2026-09-09

What September 11 Took From Me

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
During a visit in May to the Maritime Museum in Halifax, Nova Scotia, I watched a film about a 1917 cargo-ship explosion that killed more than 1,700 townspeople and injured many more. The grainy black-and-white images of the flattened city catapulted me back to the devastation of Lower Manhattan after terrorists attacked the World Trade Center on September 11, 2001. Halifax suffered a blizzard following the cargo-ship catastrophe, and the sight of the city covered in a deep blanket of snow recalled the dust and debris from the once-glimmering towers that coated the Financial District. When I heard the recorded voices of the Haligonian survivors recounting their memories, I walked out of the small theater to cry. Twenty-five years on from September 11, 2001, an image or memory can still evoke the very moment I learned that my 36-year-old redheaded husband, Jeffrey R. Smith, would not be coming home from work that day. I, too, am now a voice in a museum. At the National September 11 Memorial & Museum, any visitor can replay my family’s intensely personal memories of a national horror. Years before the museum had a building, curators teamed up with StoryCorps, an oral-history nonprofit, and tried to make it easy for families to contribute by giving us a special number to call and record from home. Making this call felt like a leap of faith. How would these recordings be used? Where would they be stored? Still, my two daughters—who were then 8 and 10 years old—and I decided we wanted to participate. Ellen S. Bakalian: Everything my husband wasn’t there for I remember sitting on the couch while the girls took turns, cupping their hands over my cellphone in a semblance of privacy. I also recall one of them wandering the house, chatting like a teenager. I remember wanting them to practice what they would say, and I wanted to hear it before they called, but my girls had their own ideas. I found it nerve-racking until I realized that whatever they said would be straight from the hearts of girls who were not yet 3 and not yet 1 when their father was killed. I haven't heard the recordings. I recently thought that I wanted to, but I couldn't imagine donning headphones and listening to my voice or my daughters’ amid strangers at the museum. I contacted the chief curator, who kindly offered a private place for me to revisit what we had said. I made a date, then canceled it. I doubt I will reschedule. I know that if I listened, the editor in me would wish we could rerecord, and the rest of me would fall apart. When we were in Nova Scotia, my friends and I took turns reading historical plaques out loud. I always make a point of reading the names on memorials wherever I find them. Years ago, one Presidents’ Day weekend, my daughters and I took the train to Washington, D.C. I remember standing in front of the Vietnam Veterans Memorial and deciding that we would read one young soldier’s name aloud as we traced our fingers over the letters. We looked up a 19-year-old soldier on a sheaf of laminated paper that was hanging on the wall and read the name of his hometown. We said a prayer for him and his family because, as I remember explaining to the girls, “one day Daddy’s name will be on a plaque somewhere, and we want people to read his name out loud.” The name Jeffrey R. Smith can be found by museumgoers in Lower Manhattan, and is also on two plaques at the University of Rochester, where Jeff earned both his undergraduate and master’s degrees. I went with my daughters to see these tributes when the girls were 18 and 16. We walked in Jeff’s footsteps. We read all of the names out loud. Unlike the men and women whose names appear on the Vietnam memorial, Jeff and the thousands of other innocent victims that Tuesday morning were not casualties of war. This is an important distinction, a line that I, like most September 11 widows and widowers, believe should not be blurred. America was senselessly and successfully attacked by terrorists, and Jeff, along with nearly 3,000 other civilians, was killed while going about an otherwise ordinary day. From the September 2026 issue: The man who refused to sit on the sidelines Knowing that our voices are in a museum because our profound personal loss is part of this country’s history is both gratifying and unsettling. I am glad that there is such a powerful monument to that awful day. I am grateful that my husband and the other innocent victims are commemorated each year with a moment of silence. But I am staggered to be part of something this big, forever and ever. On this anniversary, I think of the couples who get to experience such milestones differently—joyfully, gratefully, together. Jeff and I will never have that. This is the pain that lurks just below the surface, only to bubble up without warning, like in a museum in Halifax. I know, of course, that I am one story, one tragedy, among many. But within our national catastrophe, which changed so much about our collective understanding of security, of terror, is my own private and persistent grief.

The Wild West of Commercial Surrogacy
2026-09-09

The Wild West of Commercial Surrogacy

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
When Shoshannah Roberts talks about the four babies she’s given away, she glows. “When I think about it now, it still feels emotional,” she told me. “Those moments are so magical.” For many women, the thought of handing over a newborn to someone else is almost incomprehensible—they feel a deep revulsion at a primal sundering. But for Roberts, acting as a surrogate four times is the most meaningful thing she has done with her life. “I’ve seen the parents hold their babies right away, skin to skin. I mean, like, raw,” she said. “Baby’s off me, cord is cut, and Mom is just holding her baby and sobbing. It’s amazing.” Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. The first time I saw Roberts, who lives in Santa Barbara County, California, she was speaking at the Men Having Babies conference in downtown San Francisco. Men Having Babies does exactly what its name promises: The organization helps gay aspiring fathers find women who are willing—eager, even—to carry their children. For one weekend every year, more than 200 men from across California and beyond fill the ballroom at the Marines’ Memorial Club & Hotel and listen to panel discussions and keynotes, with a special breakout session for single dads. Since the legalization of same-sex marriage, male couples have become the big growth market for American surrogacy, but the practice is booming among aspiring parents—married or single, straight or gay—who can afford the cost of hiring someone else to carry their child. In 2024, 11,821 embryos were transferred to surrogates in American clinics associated with the Society for Assisted Reproductive Technology, up from 8,461 three years earlier, and the United Nations estimates that the global worth of the industry is almost $15 billion. Most surrogacy in America today is commercial, meaning that the carrier is paid, and gestational, meaning that the carrier has no genetic relationship to the child. Barely a week passes without another celebrity posing with their new baby on Instagram—perhaps in scrubs or doing skin-to-skin contact—and thanking their heroic surrogate. Kim Kardashian, Anderson Cooper, Meghan Trainor, and Chrissy Teigen have all used surrogates. Some celebrities, citing medical risks, hire a surrogate to take on the dangers of pregnancy for them. Others want more children than they can have biologically. Hilaria and Alec Baldwin had a sixth baby via surrogate five months after Hilaria gave birth to their fifth child. (She has since carried their seventh.) But just as paid surrogacy is becoming a more conspicuous part of American life, so, too, is the backlash. When the actor Lily Collins, then 35, announced the birth of her baby, Tove, and her “endless gratitude for our incredible surrogate,” she and her husband were bombarded with messages describing them as “human traffickers” and “baby buyers.” In the past year, multiple horror stories—fraudulent agencies, human trafficking, elderly parents who are unable to take care of a newborn—have emerged, complicating what is usually presented as an uplifting narrative. If your primary concern is reproductive freedom, most surrogacy stories will seem inspirational. But if you factor in the risks to carriers, or simply feel skeptical of unregulated commerce, your doubts will multiply. In my experience, many liberal Americans are surprised to discover that their country is an outlier in its enthusiasm for commercial surrogacy. In Britain, where I live, a ban on the practice enjoys substantial support among feminists, who worry about the power differential between paid surrogates and wealthy would-be parents. (Altruistic surrogacy, in which the surrogate carries a baby solely out of generosity, is less controversial and is permitted.) A recent UN report went even further, declaring that commercial surrogacy “constitutes the sale of children, which is a crime,” and that “surrogacy arrangements can amount to or resemble slavery.” In one recent scandal, Thai women who answered an advertisement for work as surrogates in the nation of Georgia had their passports confiscated and were coerced into donating their eggs. Several developing countries that have become hubs for surrogacy tourism have enacted laws to protect women from exploitation by wealthy foreigners. Thailand banned commercial surrogacy in 2015 after the “Baby Gammy” case, in which a surrogate was left caring for a disabled child. She alleged that she had become pregnant with twins and refused a request by the intended parents—one of whom was a convicted sex offender—to abort the twin with Down syndrome. Because so much can go wrong, every country in the European Union has banned commercial surrogacy. France’s centrist liberal president, Emmanuel Macron, has described the practice as “not compatible with the dignity of women.” Italy’s right-wing populist leader, Giorgia Meloni, has enacted one of the strictest anti-surrogacy frameworks in the world. Not only is domestic surrogacy banned, but any Italian citizen who brings home a baby born to an overseas surrogate risks jail and a fine of up to 1 million euros. Many other developed countries have either tightly restricted surrogacy or chosen, as Britain and Canada have, to allow only the altruistic version. Israel allows surrogacy but imposes a raft of conditions: The commissioning parents must not have more than two children already, must not have serious criminal convictions, and must be of the same religion as the surrogate. In general, Europeans think of Americans’ enthusiasm for paid surrogacy the way we regard their fondness for guns and capital punishment: inexplicable. The piquant difference, however, is that surrogacy is a cause of the American left rather than the right. Red states tend to have tighter limits on surrogacy. Louisiana, for example, restricts surrogacy to married heterosexual couples using their own sperm and eggs. By contrast, when New York legalized commercial surrogacy in 2020, LGBTQ activists hailed it as a triumph for “fertility equality.” The debate cuts across political traditions and defies easy categorization. When surrogacy goes well, the result is a happy family that would not otherwise have existed. When it goes wrong, however, it can do so in unpredictable and emotionally crushing ways. At its core, the ethical question is this: How much physical and emotional risk should individuals be allowed to take on in the name of a commercial transaction? California, the home of Hollywood and Silicon Valley, is the undisputed center of American surrogacy, and the practice there is infused with a distinct blend of crunchy vibes, social liberalism, and straightforward capitalism. Surrogacy costs in the state begin at about $150,000 for a single baby, according to promotional materials for a variety of agencies doing business there. The percentage paid to the surrogate varies widely. Many, many enterprises want to help make gay couples’ dreams of parenthood come true, and the Men Having Babies conference was underwritten by a number of them: IVF clinics, genetic-testing companies, and surrogacy agencies. If the sea of Zegna sneakers and stylish down jackets didn’t tell you that these guys have money, the brochures offering future fathers “white-glove service” and VIP packages made it clear. In the exhibitors’ hall, I passed an abundance of merch designed to reel clients in—candles bearing the instruction LIGHT ME WHEN MATCHED, onesies with I LOVE MY DADS on them, canvas bags with the legend DADDY VIBES, and my own favorite: multiple iterations of sperm-shaped stress balls. Some were dyed rainbow colors. This was aspirational parenthood, right here. Pictures of happy dads and adorable kids abounded, including on a banner featuring two ripped, waxed, shirtless guys draped in a Pride flag and cradling a newborn. Words such as bespoke, boutique, and concierge appeared frequently in the literature. Words such as episiotomy and mastitis did not. It was here that I learned the industry’s preferred term for a surrogate pregnancy: a journey. At its best, surrogacy showers happiness all around. Intended parents—or IPs, in industry jargon—receive a longed-for baby; their surrogate is well compensated and well treated, and has the satisfaction of knowing that she has helped create a family. A few days after the conference, Roberts told me over Zoom that she had been interested in surrogacy ever since Phoebe on Friends carried triplets for her brother, Frank. Her first experience was blissful: She carried for a single mom who met a partner midway through the pregnancy, and Roberts later attended their beach wedding, in Florida. When she went back to work at a corporate job, “it felt a little hollow,” she said. She joined the surrogacy agency Hatch as a coordinator. In addition to four surrogate pregnancies, she’s had four children of her own. “My grandmother had 12,” she said. “So I think I’m built for it.” In mid-June, she announced her ninth pregnancy—another baby of her own. In the United States, the only serious opposition to surrogacy comes from an unlikely coalition of radical feminists and socially conservative Catholics. The former see surrogacy as exploitation: In 1983’s Right-Wing Women, the feminist author Andrea Dworkin described surrogacy as part of the “brothel model” of society, where “women can sell reproductive capacities the same way old-time prostitutes sold sexual ones.” Catholics, meanwhile, have doctrinal objections to any artificial interference in pregnancy. The more typical American view, though, tends toward individualism, in line with the “My body, my choice” slogan popularized by the abortion-rights movement, and an instinctive suspicion of government regulation: If the surrogate is okay with it, what business is it of anyone else? Although you can’t legally sell a kidney—even America has its limits—consent is treated as a universal solvent for many other tricky ethical issues. Well-heeled intended parents can also dodge practical constraints faced by biological or adoptive parents. Many agencies place no hard restrictions on the number of children any single person or couple can commission. Some parents want to get to a “double-digit number,” Men Having Babies’ founder, Ron Poole-Dayan, said at the conference. In 2023, a court in Los Angeles discovered that the Chinese video-game billionaire Xu Bo was simultaneously seeking parental rights for four babies born via surrogacy and was in the process of fathering at least eight more. The children (all boys, at his request) were being raised by nannies in nearby Irvine until they could be taken to him in China, The Wall Street Journal reported. There are also no legally enforceable upper age limits on intended parents. In August 2025, two 6-year-old children were found in a caged-off section of a San Francisco apartment. Their father explained that he had commissioned twins as a single dad at the age of 67, and when one of the babies was diagnosed with severe autism, he found himself struggling to cope. The locked gate stopped the neurodivergent twin from injuring himself during tantrums or running out of the building into the road. “I’m a senior, first-time parent, single parent, to twins, without domestic help,” the 74-year-old told The San Francisco Standard after he was reported to child services. (To which I might respond: And you didn’t think about this earlier? ) Difficulties like these are why many adoption agencies suggest an upper age limit of about 50. The surrogacy industry does not have a regulator that enforces a “fit and proper person” test of the kind given to people who want to adopt a child. Last year, Pennsylvania lawmakers proposed closing a loophole in the state’s law that had allowed a sex offender—who was legally barred from adopting or fostering—to commission a baby. There are no restrictions on Americans commissioning a baby abroad, even if the other country’s health services or human-rights laws are far worse than those in the United States. America also stands virtually alone as a rich country that permits incoming surrogacy tourism from elsewhere. Many of these international intended parents are from China, where surrogacy is banned and where, until recently, family size was limited by the one-child policy. These customers have exacting standards. “They want a Caucasian surrogate to carry the baby, with preferably blond hair, blue eyes,” Dee Gulati of Surrogacy4All, in New York, told me. “And they want an egg donor who looks like a cheerleader.” The resulting babies are automatically American citizens. (Hoping to change this, President Trump in August signed an executive order denying citizenship to children born to foreign citizens through commercial surrogacy.) In the U.S., no federal laws regulate surrogacy, and contracts related to the practice are unenforceable in many states. The Society for Ethics in Egg Donation and Surrogacy provides a “self-regulation framework” for agencies, but its greatest sanction is removing membership. The lack of regulation means that success depends more on luck and personal benevolence than on industry standards or legal guardrails. The primary question asked of parents attempting this new American way of birth is: Can you afford it? For some surrogates, handing over the baby is far harder than it was for Shoshannah Roberts. Last fall, in Chattanooga, Tennessee, I met Erika Jones, a quiet, self-contained woman who wore a discreet gold cross around her neck. She told me that her three ambitions in life had always been to visit Ireland, to be a surrogate, and to see a koala in person. She had wanted to be a surrogate since she was very young, before she’d even had her own two children. A few years ago, when she found herself settled in Fairfield, Ohio, with a good job and a stable income, she registered with an agency. The story of one couple particularly moved her. The wife, whom I’ll call Priya, had lost her brother to cancer in 2020. He’d banked his sperm before he died, and his family hoped to use it to fertilize a donor egg. The resulting embryo would then be carried by a surrogate and raised by Priya and her husband, allowing a part of her brother to live on. After Jones, then 34, learned the baby’s sex, she spent $40 on a balloon filled with pink confetti and had it sent to Priya, who recorded her reaction when she opened the box. But Jones didn’t see what she had hoped to see. “You could tell that she was disappointed that it was a girl,” Jones said. “I think she wanted a boy, to closely remind her of her brother.” Would the baby inside her be treated with respect, Jones wondered—or as second-best? From that point, in Jones’s telling, the relationship between the two women began to sour. Jones found Priya fussy and controlling, and felt that she was being blamed when a scan found that the fetus was growing unusually slowly. “I was like, I’m not a slave,” she said. She even wondered briefly about trying to keep the baby, but decided that wouldn’t be right. When Jones found mold and detected carbon monoxide in her apartment, in Ohio, she decided to move back home to Tennessee, closer to her family. That would violate her 57-page “gestational carrier agreement,” in which she had agreed to “take all necessary and reasonable steps” to give birth at UC Health in West Chester, Ohio. The intended parents told her that if the baby was born in Tennessee, they would face “significant legal challenges.” Because of the state’s more restrictive laws, they feared, the contract might not be valid there. Priya and her husband suggested that Jones stay put and send her children to Tennessee to stay with a friend or relative for the remaining five months of the pregnancy. Jones declined. She moved back to Tennessee but agreed to keep traveling to Ohio for medical appointments and then give birth there. She was being paid $45,000 plus expenses for the pregnancy but found that the frequent back-and-forth between states, in addition to her time off work while sick, had eaten into that payment. She cut off direct contact with Priya and emailed the agency asking that she not attend the birth. “I know I signed the contract—I am well aware and I intend on honoring it,” Jones wrote, “but it does not need to continue to be thrown in my face every other day.” She also bought a puppy, aware that she would soon be coming home from the hospital empty-handed; she wanted, she later wrote in a memoir, something “alive, soft and ours.” When Jones went into labor, she brought her two children, then ages 10 and 12, to the hospital, along with Susan Harris, a trusted friend, who alerted Priya and her husband. The labor was long—37 hours, 22 of them without an epidural. When Jones told the midwives that she wanted to push, the room suddenly filled with people. Earlier in her labor, Jones had asked for five minutes with the baby before handing her over. But the moment the little girl was delivered, she said, someone shouted, “No contact!” All she saw was a glimpse of dark-brown hair as the baby was taken away. She was not even told her birth weight. (Harris confirmed this account.) Priya told The Atlantic that she wasn’t “in any way disappointed” that the baby was a girl. She felt that because Jones had signed a legal contract agreeing to not have any skin-to-skin contact with the baby, she didn’t have to honor Jones’s request to hold her. “Maybe if you were nicer,” she thought. She has since ignored Jones’s request to talk through what happened. “At the end of the day, this is a business. We’re not friends,” she told The Atlantic. “You’re not doing me a favor. You’re getting paid $5,000 a month.” Both parties had fulfilled their side of the contract, she said, and it was time to move on. Jones has not found that so easy. She told me that the experience kept playing in her mind: “I felt like I was treated less than a human, and less than an animal.” According to the industry, unhappy surrogates are extremely rare. The owner of the surrogacy agency that Priya used told me that Jones’s experience was an “outlier,” one of only three cases out of the hundreds she had overseen that had “gone south.” At the Men Having Babies conference, an agency director assured the attendees that almost all of the “horror stories” reported in the press involved traditional surrogacy, in which the woman carrying the baby uses her own egg. (She added that the traditional approach now accounts for only 5 percent of American surrogacies.) The sales pitch to intended parents for gestational surrogacy—involving an egg from either the commissioning mother or a separate donor—is precisely that the surrogate has no legal claim to custody. That makes it legally safer for the rich couples who pay the bills than a traditional surrogacy. But it may be more physically dangerous for the surrogate. At the Men Having Babies conference, the first audience question was a stark one: How many surrogates die giving birth? Deaths are very rare, Poole-Dayan, the organizer, assured them. A 2020 study found that gestational surrogates were no more likely to die in childbirth than any other pregnant woman. Yet early evidence suggests that carrying a child who is not genetically related to you could increase the risk of complications: A 2024 study of more than 850,000 pregnancies over a decade in Ontario found that “severe maternal morbidity and adverse pregnancy outcomes” were three times higher among gestational carriers than in conventional pregnancies. When the mother and the fetus are not genetically related, the developmental biologist Emma Hilton has written, “Mum’s immune system may ramp up to fight against the wholly foreign tissue. In response, placental invasion becomes more aggressive.” (A systematic review from 2015 found that “most studies reporting on surrogacy have serious methodological limitations.”) Because of the elevated risks associated with twin pregnancies, most reputable agencies now implant only one embryo at a time, but—a recurring theme in this story—no law or professional regulation requires them to do so. To Poole-Dayan, the value of a contract and of compensation is that it forces both parties to have uncomfortable conversations while the risks are still theoretical. “I always tell people, ‘Imagine yourself at the funeral of the surrogate,’ ” he told me. I found Poole-Dayan’s forthrightness a refreshing change from the cloying online culture around surrogacy, with the staged hospital shots and cutesy euphemisms designed to play down the toll of pregnancy and birth. In one video, a little girl born via surrogacy describes the woman who carried her—who built her, cell by cell, inside her own body—as an “Easy-Bake Oven.” When Jones concluded that she had been mistreated, she turned to Kallie Fell. A perinatal nurse by training, Fell works out of an office the size of a janitor’s closet in a co-working space in Pleasant Hill, east of San Francisco. Fell is the executive director of the Center for Bioethics and Culture Network, which was founded in 2000 to campaign against “Big Fertility” but in recent years has also opposed youth gender transition. The CBC has only two full-time employees; Fell told me that it is “lonely work.” Her inbox is full of questions, unhappy stories, and requests for legal help: a surrogate left with medical debt after the intended parents ghosted her, a man who regrets being a sperm donor, women who don’t want to abort a fetus with disabilities but have signed a contract promising to do so. “I’m a little bit of a magnet,” she said. One of the most frequent problems, Fell told me, is the financial imbalance between a surrogate and the intended parents. Ethical agencies will ask the IPs to pay for an independent lawyer to read the contract on their surrogate’s behalf, but no law or regulation enforces this. Contracts therefore tend to be written with the protection of the IPs in mind rather than the carrier. Surrogates can be asked to sign nondisclosure agreements, to protect the privacy of the IPs and the child, but these can also prevent them from speaking honestly about bad experiences. Mental-health troubles can be grounds for disqualification for potential surrogates, but IPs typically face less scrutiny. Last year, Wired reported on a woman with bipolar disorder who blamed her surrogate for having a stillbirth; in a fury, she sent a photo of the corpse to an iPad belonging to the surrogate’s 7-year-old son. The same imbalance is true of criminal records: Another woman who’d contacted Fell, Amanda Misbahu, told me that midway through her surrogate pregnancy—arranged via Facebook—she began to worry that the IP, a gay single father, hadn’t been entirely honest about his criminal history. To me, Misbahu’s story sounded like a surrogacy nightmare, but she cheerfully insisted that she would be willing to act as a surrogate again. Misbahu’s upbringing had been turbulent. She had been taken away from her own mother when she was six months old, she said, with “maggots in my diapers and Pepsi in my bottles,” and had been raised by a traditionally minded, religious family. The turmoil continued into her adulthood, in successive marriages that didn’t work out. By age 23, Misbahu had given birth to five babies, but none of them lived with her full-time; some were being raised primarily by relatives. In her 30s, her thoughts turned to surrogacy. “It was a way for me, in a sentence, to help me through some of my own trauma,” she said. Giving up her own children had left her distraught, she said, yet “with this one, I knew the outcome, I knew it was going to happen, and it was a situation that I knew would be traumatic but I could control.” Two agencies turned her down: the first because of her weight, and the second after her early psychological trauma came up in a medical screening. So she went on Facebook to advertise her services directly to would-be parents. When a man whom I’ll call Cameron got in contact with Misbahu, she wrote her own surrogacy contract, and “he let me pick the egg donor—it was like online dating,” she said. She and Cameron wanted two embryos transferred, but the fertility doctor advised only one. “We said, We’ll take our $100,000 and we’ll go next door,” she told me. The doctor relented but made her sign a waiver. Only one of the embryos implanted. At eight weeks, she was hospitalized for life-threatening blood clots. She needed blood thinners for the rest of her pregnancy. “I thought she was gonna die,” her fourth child, Alyssa, told me. “So I would just start freaking out randomly when I thought about it—like, at school, sometimes I’d be crying in lunch.” Although Misbahu didn’t know it, Cameron had once pleaded no contest to involuntary manslaughter in California after injecting a friend with a fatal dose of Benadryl in his apartment. He then moved to Kentucky and practiced as a physician’s assistant without a license, a deception that came to light after patients claimed that he had botched their cosmetic surgeries. In 2001, he’d been sentenced to 20 years in prison for five counts of second-degree assault and 112 counts of wanton endangerment, although he was released early. In learning about his past, Misbahu also discovered that he had an adult daughter. In the end, she concluded that he’d turned his life around and that there was nothing she could do except go through with the contract. Cameron and his new boyfriend were present for the birth, for which Misbahu had an epidural for the first time in her history of deliveries, “because I’m like, I don’t want to have to feel any of this,” she told me. She cried—from the hormones, she recalled—and saw the baby a few hours later: “And I was like, She’s so pretty, but I really just can’t even touch her.” Misbahu attended counseling sessions for six months after the birth. “You felt like you lost a baby,” she said. “And I wouldn’t stop pumping milk.” Eventually, a friend told her that she had to move on, and so she donated the breast milk to a local bank for premature babies. Her counselor wasn’t much help, she said: “She thought it was the coolest thing; she had never met a surrogate. She just wanted me to paint pictures in her office. And I was like, Ma’am, I feel like I want to blow out my fucking brains.” Misbahu told me that she even gave her gun to her brother because she was so worried about her mental health. A few months after we first spoke, I met Misbahu in a pancake restaurant on the outskirts of Columbus, Ohio. She turned up bearing a knitted kangaroo that her mother had made for me, and she wore a medical-alert bracelet around her wrist. Her clotting condition had convinced her that she could not advertise herself as a surrogate again. However, she had married a Ghanaian asylum seeker whom she’d met on a dating app, and was trying for a baby with him to strengthen his claim. (They have since split.) For a time, Misbahu had remained in contact with Cameron—and had even helped him find his next surrogate. But eventually, their relationship broke down. She had asked him to buy a present for her daughter, and he’d told her that she couldn’t treat him as an ATM. “Finally, I wrote him back, and I was like, Look, I almost literally died giving birth to your kid. Like, it doesn’t mean you owe me anything. But that was pretty disrespectful,” she told me. (Cameron did not respond to questions from The Atlantic.) He blocked her on Facebook. But one of her friends could still see his page, so she used that account to secretly watch his daughter—the baby she had carried—grow up. She showed me a photo of the little girl and her siblings on her phone. “The girls are so pretty,” she said with pride. Surrogacy has a supply problem. There are not enough ethically motivated, healthy, middle-class American women to meet demand. Speakers at the Men Having Babies conference repeatedly stressed that reputable agencies accept only 5 percent of applicants as surrogates. Good agencies screen women for health and psychological problems, and question them about their finances and the supportiveness of their partner. Those safeguards are reflected in the high costs. Look abroad, though, and it’s a different story. Some agencies offer “guaranteed baby” packages for as little as $50,000. Surrogacy destinations pop up anywhere the local laws are lax, such as Northern Cyprus, a territory that exists in a legal limbo and is recognized only by Turkey. (The problem is that babies born there can end up stateless.) For parents from Russia, nearby Georgia is a similar no-questions-asked destination. India used to be the most favored low-cost destination, but in 2015, Prime Minister Narendra Modi, citing fears of exploitation, banned commercial surrogacy for foreigners. Ukraine’s appeal as a surrogacy destination has diminished since Russia’s invasion in 2022, although the leading agency there, BioTexCom, told me that the company is still handing off one baby born via surrogacy every day on average, at a cost of about $60,000. A representative of the agency initially told me that it was planning to open a clinic in Kazakhstan, to cater to the growing Asian market. In July, however, that country abruptly banned foreign surrogacy amid concerns about exploitation. BioTexCom takes a more unsentimental approach to surrogacy than American agencies. When I asked Denis Herman, the legal director, what his best day on the job had been, I expected him to tell me about uniting a baby with happy parents. Instead, he talked about business metrics: “I’m really focused on the KPIs, on getting the milestones done.” BioTexCom’s founder, Albert Tochilovsky, told me that he worried about falling birth rates, and presented surrogacy as an alternative to immigration. BioTexCom agreed to let me speak with one of its current surrogates, a 40-year-old whom it identified as Nana, and assigned an employee to translate our conversation. This was Nana’s third surrogate pregnancy since 2021, and she had two much older children of her own. During the first surrogate pregnancy, she was given an apartment to live in with her kids, she told me, so she’d decided to do it twice more; it was her only job. (Compensation for BioTexCom surrogates begins at $23,000, according to the company. The average yearly salary in Ukraine is about $8,700.) The image that Nana and the agency presented was of surrogacy as a sensible economic option and a morally good one. “In Ukraine, we say: Children are a flower of our life,” the translator told me. Mexico is more convenient for Americans, and a journey might cost $100,000 less there than in the States. But babies born in Mexico through surrogacy are given a Mexican passport by default, which can cause problems at the U.S. border. The State Department warns Americans that “there is no legal framework to protect parents who pursue surrogacy in Mexico, despite agencies and clinics promoting Mexico as a safe, legal destination for commercial surrogacy.” Wes Johnson-Ellis of the British firm My Surrogacy Journey has sufficient faith in the Mexican system that he and his husband, Michael, had their third child through a surrogate in Mexico City. Yet he acknowledges the concern that carriers in the country are taking on medical risk out of economic need. “Let’s not be naive,” Johnson-Ellis told me. “In Mexico, the compensation that we offer is three times the average wage, so it is a significant amount of money for them.” He estimated that his team accepts only 20 percent of the women who apply to be surrogates, and he said that they filter out anyone who is motivated only by money. Several surrogacy agencies told me that they worry deeply about the booming market in Mexico, and Johnson-Ellis expressed doubts about his company’s competitors there. “They don’t operate in an ethical way,” he said. “They don’t safeguard everyone; they don’t look after everyone.” Gulati, the practice director of Surrogacy4All, which arranges approximately 200 surrogate pregnancies every year, said that his favored destination for a low-cost journey is now Ghana, where he charges intended parents a flat rate of $49,000. Those carriers are under close watch. “The great thing about doing surrogacy in Ghana is that they live in a dormitory which is right near the hospital, so they can’t drink, they can’t smoke, they can’t do drugs,” he said. “During that period of time, they are monitored 24 hours, seven days a week. And as a result, the success rate with surrogacy is very, very high.” Even in California, the regulation of agencies leaves much to be desired. In May 2025, the caregivers of a two-month-old baby were reported to social services for potential abuse. When the police raided their mansion in Arcadia, a small city northeast of Los Angeles, they discovered another 15 children, who were being homeschooled by a platoon of nannies. “There were probably anywhere from six to eight at the time that we were there,” Captain Kollin Cieadlo of the Arcadia Police Department told me. Footage from surveillance cameras showed that the children were being drilled in English and Mandarin and forced, as a form of discipline, to do squats and other physical exercises that the police considered “above and beyond what a normal punishment would be.” Further searches revealed six more kids whose birth certificates declared them to also be the children of Silvia Zhang and Guojun Xuan, who owned the house. The couple had been running an agency, Mark Surrogacy, with only one client—themselves. In 2023, one of their surrogates had been obligated, thanks to the prebirth order she had signed, to hand over a newly delivered child to people she had never seen before, who arrived at the hospital with power-of-attorney documents. The sheer number of parental orders obtained by Zhang and Xuan during this period triggered an investigation ordered by a judge, but no further action was taken. As news of the mansion raid spread, surrogates then currently under contract with Mark Surrogacy—and still pregnant—began finding one another in Facebook groups. They all told similar stories, of being commissioned by a woman who said that she was unable to conceive and desperately wanted a child. When Zhang and Xuan were questioned by the police, they denied that anything was amiss. “They simply said they just wanted a large family with a lot of kids,” Cieadlo said. The children went into foster care, and one of the surrogates, Kayla Elliott of Corpus Christi, Texas, started a GoFundMe to try to win custody of the child she’d carried. (She has since said that she believes the baby should be left with the foster parents, for continuity of care.) No charges have been filed against Zhang and Xuan, who have since commissioned even more babies. Although they could face police action over the allegations of child abuse, the startling fact is that their baby-creation spree is legal in California. (Their lawyers did not respond to a request for comment.) The Wall Street Journal reported that the police were investigating the couple for possible trafficking, but Zhang told the newspaper that she intended to raise all of the children: “We never sell our babies. We take care of them very well.” After speaking with Jones and Misbahu, I spent hours turning over their stories in my mind, trying to reconcile their bad experiences with the many upbeat counterexamples I had seen at the Men Having Babies conference. None of those women had needed surrogacy to stay solvent, and more than one had joked to me that they could have earned more as an Uber driver. For Veronica Bowers, a two-time surrogate who now works for the Northwest Surrogacy Center in Oregon, surrogacy was a way for her to be with her own kids while still contributing to the household finances. (For similar reasons, military wives are actively recruited by surrogacy agencies.) “It is a badge of honor,” Bowers told me. “I feel really proud that I did this.” The happiest surrogacy experiences I saw resembled open adoptions, where the process is transparent and the birth mother can stay in touch with the child. (Bowers and Roberts are in contact with the children they carried—like an aunt rather than a mother.) The bad stories involved parents who behaved as though they were ordering a baby from a mail-order catalog. I was also left deeply uneasy by the realities of international surrogacy: Why should California be a place where Chinese parents go to find blond, blue-eyed surrogates for status reasons—and why should American parents have the right to acquire a cut-rate child by employing a Ghanaian woman to live in a dormitory under 24/7 monitoring? To protect everyone involved, states should impose upper limits on the age of commissioning parents and on the number of children any couple or individual can commission, as well as an equalization of the disclosure requirements. If surrogates must reveal their medical and criminal histories, then so should the rich people employing them. Something that surprised me was that many of the women I interviewed who had found surrogacy to be a traumatic experience were ambivalent, rather than flatly opposed, to the practice. Jones said that she hadn’t ruled out another journey, although she was still suffering medical complications from the difficult pregnancy and birth. She also worried that the baby girl she had carried would never know “three pertinent people in her life”—the biological father who had died before she was conceived, the egg donor who had provided half of her DNA, and the surrogate who had carried her for nine nausea-filled months. Jones had watched videos online of children born through surrogacy who opposed the practice, and she wondered whether she had been selfless to become a surrogate—or selfish. At the end of our meeting, she said—just as Amanda Misbahu did—that she had carried on pumping breast milk for months after the birth, even though she’d had no baby to nourish. She had also asked friends to check Priya’s social media to see what the baby she’d carried looked like now. On her phone, she showed me a photo of the little girl smiling alongside Priya’s other children. Other than that picture, Jones said, her hospital bracelet and blanket are her only mementos of carrying the girl. She still feels the loss: “Truthfully, even though she’s not my child, she’s my child.” This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “Mothers for Sale.” When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

Medicine Needs to Get Serious About AI
2026-09-08

Medicine Needs to Get Serious About AI

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
For as long as ChatGPT has existed, Americans have been asking it, “Could that funny little spot on my toe be cancer?” And as AI has advanced over the past few years, doctors have begun to use specialized models to ask much the same questions in clinics. In the face of these developments, professional medical societies have presented a unified front: Doctors must always have the final say in clinical care. We disagree. As the medical societies allow, physicians’ enhancing their work with AI tools may be a perfectly good way to deliver medicine. (One of us—Khosla—has in fact invested in a primary-care start-up, co-founded by his son, in which doctors supervise AI assistants.) But for Americans to get the best health care possible, we need to be willing to experiment with models that put AI in control. This notion has caused a stir in the medical community. When we published a commentary in the Journal of the American Medical Association last month arguing that AI-only care could soon become superior to care provided by human physicians, the CEO of the American Medical Association denounced us as undermining the patient-doctor relationship. The organization’s position was unchanged: AI should be relegated to a supporting role in clinical care. It’s an argument that ignores the available evidence and could hold untold numbers of Americans back from better health. Much of modern medicine—including managing hypertension, determining the right treatment for many cancers, and addressing chemotherapy side effects—is largely algorithmic. A physician needs to ask a patient targeted questions, determine possible diagnoses, order tests to identify the correct one, offer a treatment plan, and adjust it depending on how the patient responds. That series of actions is steered by detailed guidelines from professional societies. Yet doctors—despite the best of intentions—make plenty of mistakes when following those steps. Studies indicate that up to 15 percent of American patients are misdiagnosed each year, leading to tens of thousands of preventable deaths. That’s why health-care providers have software that notifies them if they, say, prescribe a deadly dose of a drug. Any software that could practice medicine autonomously would be similarly specialized: not generic ChatGPT, but a model trained in medical knowledge with the appropriate guardrails to minimize risks and mistakes. Such a model could check for drug interactions more consistently and comprehensively than physicians. And it would be able to check in with patients much more frequently than human health-care providers do. Some AI models have already proved adept at such tasks. In one study published last year, expert physicians judged Google’s Articulate Medical Intelligence Explorer—a highly trained AI model for medical diagnosis and reasoning—as significantly better than human doctors at getting actors playing patients to open up. The “patients” revealed more about their complaints, along with their medical, family, and medication history, all of which can be crucial for an accurate diagnosis. In a 2023 study, patients with type 2 diabetes were randomized to have their insulin dose adjusted by physicians or autonomous AI. The machines got patients to a stable dose faster than human physicians, and their patients took their insulin more reliably and had less distress related to their diabetes. What about the physician-AI hybrids that medical associations are backing? Research on them is sparse, and more is needed. A 2025 review of 52 studies concluded that, on balance, such hybrids “neither outperformed medical AI alone nor surpassed the best of clinicians.” In at least a few of those studies, human-AI hybrids outperformed AI alone. But in those hybrid teams, the AI controlled the final clinical decision—the opposite of what the AMA and others have in mind. Medical organizations are not entirely closed-minded to the possibility of change. For example, last week the American College of Physicians published a position paper that allowed that “fully autonomous AI is possible,” but cautioned that its use should be infrequent and “limited to low-risk, low-complexity decisions, and always allow for bringing a clinician into the loop.” John Whyte, the CEO of the AMA, told The Atlantic that he was “disappointed” in the quality of the analysis in our JAMA commentary last month. He questioned the design of the studies we reviewed, many of which involved simulated situations with actors or a retrospective review of existing cases rather than real clinical outcomes, and did not follow patients for years or decades. We agree that longer-term, real-world studies are necessary; hopefully, once advanced AI medical tools have existed for more than a handful of years, results will begin to arrive. Whyte was enthusiastic about further study of physicians controlling AI tools, but only cautiously open to further exploration of autonomous AI in medicine, arguing that the latter would need to be highly regulated. Reasons exist, of course, to be skeptical of AI-driven medical care. In nine of the 51 studies we reviewed, human physicians outperformed the machines. But some of these tested older AI models or general-use AI models that weren’t trained on medical information and specific guardrails for patient care. Prominent physicians have also argued that real-life doctors are harried by 15-minute appointments and administrative burdens such as prior authorization. That is certainly true, but it is also irrelevant to many of the experiments we reviewed, which examined relatively hassle-free simulations of medical appointments. One of the most common objections to an AI-doctor future is that robots lack the human connection that patients need to build trust. The American College of Physicians’ recent position paper, for example, cited AI’s inability to make eye contact. But consider that doctors, too, can be robotic. In a Gallup survey conducted late last year, 21 percent of respondents said they had turned to AI because they had previously felt dismissed by a health-care provider. In the 2025 study of Google’s AMIE, patient actors reported that the AI was more emotionally supportive than the physicians were. In a systematic review of 15 studies comparing AI chatbots with physicians, 13 found that AI had significantly higher perceived empathy. Like human physicians, AI is not and never will be perfect. But isn’t the possibility of reducing fatalities caused by physician error—even by just a little—worth exploring? Instead of writing off a possible source of progress, the medical community should help answer the key questions raised by AI: How should researchers devise ethical, real-world situations to test autonomous AI models? How should governments and professional organizations regulate them? And how should medical education be reformed so that human doctors can continue to do what they uniquely can? Hundreds of years ago, doctors believed that disease was caused by an imbalance of “humors” in the body. Even after Louis Pasteur proved that bacteria caused infection and Joseph Lister introduced sterile surgical techniques, physicians cut patients to have them bleed out what ailed them, not bothering to clean the knife or their hands. But medicine progresses. Humans have learned from our past mistakes and created new drugs, procedures, and treatments to massively lengthen lifespan and improve quality of life. Today need not be any different. Doctors have been presented with a powerful tool that has the potential to improve care for millions of people. It’s their duty not to stand in the way.
