Cereal Has Strayed Too Far
2026-09-08

Cereal Has Strayed Too Far

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Breakfast-cereal boxes are a window into both the politics of food and American culture. As a particularly timely example, since Robert F. Kennedy Jr. took office last year as secretary of health and human services, he has called for the removal of artificial dyes from breakfast cereals. On Fox News in 2024, he lamented that the bright colors in Froot Loops are “literally poisoning our kids.” (How exactly these dyes interact with human health is complicated.) Cereal companies responded right away. General Mills announced last month that it had transitioned its entire U.S. portfolio of cereals—including Trix and Lucky Charms—to all-natural dyes made from fruits, veggies, and spices. Kellogg had previously announced plans to do the same by the end of 2027. Target stopped selling any cereals with artificial colors earlier this year. For the Make America Healthy Again movement, the removal of artificial dyes is a big win. But cereal still comes with a major concern for many consumers. Nearly all ready-to-eat breakfast cereals sold in the United States—and especially those marketed to children—are presweetened. The initial loading of breakfast cereals with sugar has been attributed to a single person: Jim Rex, a heating-equipment salesman who lived in Pennsylvania in the early 20th century. As the story goes, Rex was appalled by the spoonfuls of sugar his kids added to their breakfast cereals and set out to create a version that would not induce such behavior. His brilliant contribution: a glossy honey coating on puffed wheat, a cereal he named Ranger Joe Wheat Honnies in 1939. It didn’t take long for Rex’s strategy to backfire. By 1949, Post had launched Sugar Crisp. Kellogg introduced Sugar Pops in 1951, Sugar Frosted Flakes in 1952, and Froot Loops in 1963, each among the company’s all-time best sellers. General Mills launched Sugar Smiles in 1953. The next year, the company added sugar and artificial colors to its Kix cereal, thus creating Trix. In 1950, such presweetened cereals represented just 5 percent of all cereals sold, but by the mid-1970s, kids accounted for almost 40 percent of all cereal consumption. In 2014, the Environmental Working Group estimated that kids could ingest about nine pounds of sugar a year just by eating a serving of cereal every day. Advocates for children’s health have long complained about the high sugar content of breakfast cereals. In the early 1970s, Congress held hearings to highlight cereal’s inadequate nutritional quality. Cereal companies objected, arguing, correctly, that grains are nutritious. But they also responded by trying to make their products appear less sugary. They began by removing the S-word from cereal names, and some substituted honey for sugar on the theory that people perceive honey as more wholesome and nutritious. In the 1990s, companies hit on another clever strategy: If they used multiple sugar types, they could list grains first on a product’s ingredients but sprinkle sugars throughout the list. Cereals looked healthier, but when Consumer Reports compared the sugars in 27 breakfast cereals in 2008, it found that many were still 50 percent sugar by weight. Rumors circulated that federal agencies were working to set nutrition standards for foods advertised on television programs aimed at children younger than 12, which would likely spell trouble for sugary cereals. In 2009, General Mills pledged to reduce sugar in its most popular kids’ cereals—most of which had at least 12 grams a serving—to single digits. Its pledge would apply to 10 cereals, including some of its most popular, such as Lucky Charms and Cocoa Puffs. The results were mixed. By 2012, General Mills had reached the single-digit goal for Cinnamon Toast Crunch, Cookie Crisp, and more. Some of this success was due to decreased serving size. And the best General Mills could do for Lucky Charms was 10 grams of sugar in a three-quarters-cup serving—which the company found to be the minimum sweet spot for kids’ acceptance. The project had encountered a harsh reality: Kids like sugar, and companies remove it at their peril. Sugary cereal is not just a consumer concern; it is also a matter of government nutrition policy. Since 1980, the federal Special Supplemental Nutrition Program for Women, Infants, and Children (WIC) has limited the sugars in cereals it will cover to six grams an ounce, about half of the amount in typical cereals aimed at kids. WIC can be a lucrative business for cereals that qualify, so the limit has induced companies to take two actions. The first is to formulate new cereals with less sugar specifically for the WIC market. In 2006, for example, General Mills introduced a new kid-targeted cereal, Dora the Explorer, “lightly sweetened cinnamon corn puffs,” with six grams of sugar in a 27-gram serving—an amount that meets the WIC standards. The second is to petition to change how sugar in cereals is counted. When the six-gram rule went into effect, in the early 1980s, Kellogg immediately objected. Its Bran Flakes met the WIC standards, but Raisin Bran did not; it contained 12 grams of sugar an ounce, seven from the raisins. Kellogg argued that raisins are healthy and pointed out that WIC was encouraging women to add raisins to their diet to increase their iron intake. For decades, despite Kellogg’s persistent efforts, the U.S. Department of Agriculture did not budge. But times change. In 2024, USDA issued its third comprehensive update to the WIC food package since 1975. The agency retained the six-gram limit but specified that it applied only to added, not total, sugars. It said the policy change was justified to align with listing “added sugars” on food labels and with the government publication Dietary Guidelines for Americans, and to create a “more permissive standard for products containing naturally occurring sugars.” The distinction between natural and added sugars has continued under Kennedy. The new dietary guidelines, released in January, declare that no amount of added sugar is recommended for healthy eating. At a press conference announcing the new guidelines, Kennedy told reporters, “Today, our government declares war on added sugar.” Current breakfast cereals have less sugar than they did years ago, but it’s hard to remove completely. Some amount of sugar is still needed to mask the bitter taste of added vitamins. Sugar also helps cereal flakes, puffs, and Os hold their shape in milk. Less sugar is better for kids’ health, but more is better for structural integrity—and taste. For a truly healthy cereal, look to a time before Jim Rex. The most nutritious option will have one ingredient: wheat or oats. Even if you add your own teaspoon of sugar, it will be only four grams. This article has been adapted from Marion Nestle and Lisa Sutherland’s new book, Sugar Coated. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

Buddy...when the original Star Trek ran, it was a different world, especially in the case of what was allowed on television. The bedrooms of married couples showed twin beds. The first interracial kiss in US television history occurred on Star Trek, and that episode was banned across much of the South. Women were not considered worthy of positions of authority, especially if it included having authority over men. It was literally a different world. Until the advent of Star Trek, "science fiction" on television consisted of simple-minded fantasy series like Lost In Space, Time Tunnel, Land of the Giants, etc. Tropes such as faster-than-light travel and interstellar conflict, which Babylon 5 relied upon, barely existed before Star Trek. Even sub-plots that carried on for whole seasons were rare. Episodes were expected to stand alone. Let me put it this way: The original Star Trek swam in very different waters than Babylon 5, and it changed the rules in ways that allowed Babylon 5 to be what it was (which was excellent, by the way). News and Politics - Slate Magazine
We’re Pretty Sure We Know Who’s Going to Win the House
2026-09-09

We’re Pretty Sure We Know Who’s Going to Win the House

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. With the primaries mostly behind us, general-election season is here. We at the Slatest have been covering the midterms for a while, but getting wrapped up in individual races risks overlooking the more important question: Which party will control Congress once the dust has settled? It’s a big topic, so we’re taking it one chamber at a time. Later this week we’ll consider the Senate, but today we’re focusing on the House. OK, let’s start with the obvious: Which party is going to win? I don’t have a crystal ball, but history is instructive. In the modern era, the sitting president’s party almost always loses House seats in midterms. The opposite has happened only a few times: during the depths of the Great Depression, after Republicans overreached by moving to impeach Bill Clinton, and after the Sept. 11 attacks. On average, over the past 92 years of midterms, the party in power has lost 27 seats. Republicans are currently sitting on a single-digit majority. Doesn’t that mean that Democrats are a shoo-in? The odds are in their favor. Republicans are contending with high gas prices (thanks to Donald Trump’s war in Iran), voter discontent over the cost of living (thanks in part to Trump’s tariffs), and a vainglorious but deeply unpopular president. Democrats lead by about 6 points on the generic ballot, which measures which party voters prefer without taking individual candidates into consideration. Republicans are, dare we say, in a bit of disarray. As Politico reported last week, some GOP candidates representing harder-to-win districts don’t want the president to campaign for them this fall. Others, as my colleague Jim Newell explains, are anxious that Trump’s super PAC won’t spend money to help them except on ads that would yoke them to his unpopular record. There are a few reasons to think Democrats’ path to a majority may be rocky. Redistricting is one of them. Over the past couple of years, Republicans have broken with long-standing precedent and frenziedly redrawn their states’ congressional maps in a bid to advantage GOP candidates. (Democrats have responded in kind, though it’s worth remembering that the arms race kicked off in earnest when Trump demanded that Texas further gerrymander that state’s maps.) It hasn’t always worked; last week, Missouri’s Supreme Court blocked the state from using a more-GOP-friendly map in November. But as things stand, winning the House will probably be more of an uphill climb for Democrats than it otherwise would’ve been. Democrats are barreling toward Election Day with some liabilities of their own. Joe Biden’s presidency left a bad taste in most Americans’ mouths, and the party remains broadly unpopular. Many Democratic voters are unhappy with their party’s established leaders, and debates over support for Israel have riven the party’s base. In key Maine, Michigan, and California swing seats, Democrats have gambled on progressive nominees who may prove to be too far left for centrist electorates. So you’re saying Republicans have a chance? To be clear, it would be shocking if Democrats failed to win a House majority. But the margin also matters. A slim majority is harder to corral because a few lawmakers can hold the rest hostage to extract concessions. Unexpected resignations or deaths, a phenomenon both parties have dealt with this year, can become hugely consequential when only a few seats determine House control. Still, politics moves at light speed these days, and a lot could happen before most voters head to the polls. If Trump’s trade war or war in Iran winds down, it could boost GOP House candidates. But given both historical trends and present circumstances, Democrats are clear favorites to retake control. The Senate, as we’ll explore soon, is a much closer call. OK, that’s enough forecasting for one day. Let’s talk about something closer at hand—like how to spend the rest of your evening. My colleagues recommend: A suggestion for schools: As summer vacation comes to a close, students will likely transition to spending most of their time indoors, looking at screens. But what if they could have more unstructured time outdoors? Natalie Pattillo makes the case that recess is an easy way for students—especially teenagers—to unplug from their devices, reset their minds, and build their relationships. Some self-care advice: Finch, a popular wellness and habit-tracking app, promises to help users achieve their self-care goals with the assistance of a colorful cartoon bird. “It’s Tamagotchi for the burned out and overwhelmed,” writes Sara Jin Li. But it turns out that more screen time is not a great solution to feeling burned out and overwhelmed. Instead, there’s an easier, analog method that can do the trick. An old book to revisit: Octavia Butler fans, rejoice! After being out of print for decades, the author’s third novel, Survivor, has just been rereleased. Slate writer Rebecca Onion spoke with a scholar of Butler’s work about why science-fiction fans should read this book. A new book to explore: Marlon James’ new book, The Disappearers, follows a group of gay men grappling with extreme homophobia in 1980s Jamaica. It has already been called the “novel of the decade”—and it lives up to the hype, according to Slate critic Laura Miller. She writes, “The masterstroke of The Disappearers is that James lands the finish, a feat of generosity, compassion, and, as with the rest of this unforgettable book, unconquerable bravado.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. If you want to block ads but still support Slate, consider subscribing. Already a member? Sign in here
News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Democrats Keep Looking for the “Next Obama.” They Should Do This Instead.
2026-09-09

Democrats Keep Looking for the “Next Obama.” They Should Do This Instead.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first time I remember hearing Barack Obama’s name was in late 2006 or early 2007. I was sitting in seventh grade social studies, where we were encouraged to discuss current events. “I think he’s going to run for president,” our teacher confided in a knowing yet slightly hushed tone, almost like she still couldn’t quite believe it. Back then, much of the excitement was bound up in the prospect of America having its first Black president, but Obama ultimately proved himself to be worthy of it. Two decades on, the energy he created has morphed into something decidedly more desperate. Obama is the only Democratic president this century to win reelection and remains one of the country’s most popular political figures. Which might explain why Democrats, looking ahead to congressional elections this fall and a 2028 presidential race whose stakes feel existential, keep looking for the former president’s spiritual successor: someone who can inspire, speak, and above all win, just like the real thing. All summer, voters, consultants, and pundits have repeatedly attached Obama’s name like a Homeric epithet to a bumper crop of young, talented, and mostly male candidates running across the country. It’s happened to Abdul El-Sayed, the party’s nominee for U.S. Senate in Michigan, whose self-confident swagger and immigrant-family story have invited the comparison. “It reminded me a lot of the Barack Obama bio,” Jon Favreau, Obama’s former speechwriter, recalled after seeing El-Sayed at a fundraiser. Then there’s James Talarico, the Texas state representative running for U.S. Senate whose winning primary campaign former New York Times columnist Charles Blow likened to Obama’s 2008 faceoff against Hillary Clinton. After Talarico’s campaign posted footage of the former president stumping with him at a taco shop this spring, the equivalencies flowed in. “I think you have what it takes to be the next Obama and Lord knows we need it!” one Facebook commenter wrote. The Obama parallel has similarly been lavished on two Democratic governors who look set to cruise to reelection this fall: Josh Shapiro of Pennsylvania, and Wes Moore of Maryland, who started fielding Obama analogies even before he became the state’s first Black governor. Pete Buttigieg, the former Biden transportation secretary who isn’t officially running for anything right now, has been getting the comparison for years. Even Graham Platner, the scruffy Maine oyster farmer, was winning Obama-esque praise before his Senate candidacy imploded. Jim Messina, Obama’s 2012 campaign manager, dubbed Platner’s victory speech on primary night a “masterclass” that recalled the 44th president. (A few weeks later, after an ex-girlfriend accused Platner of rape, Messina suggested Platner should “go jump in a Maine lake.”) Further south, Sen. Jon Ossoff, a Georgia Democrat who is up for reelection this fall, has been likened to the former president for viral speeches that are already earning him national attention and fueling 2028 chatter. The search for an Obama inheritor isn’t totally new. Ahead of the 2020 election, Joe Biden, Kamala Harris, and Cory Booker tussled over the former president’s legacy in what the Los Angeles Times perceptively dubbed “the Obama primary.” The search has taken on new proportions and greater urgency as Democrats once again seek to claw their way back to power after yet another Trump presidency. The question is: Even if they find their guy, is an Obama 2.0 what the party needs, now, or in 2028? To give Democrats their due, trying to re-create a popular two-term commander in chief in the aggregate doesn’t seem like the worst strategy in the world. After the Biden interregnum and Donald Trump’s return, nostalgia for better political times appears broadly felt: A January poll of a hypothetical 2028 third-term matchup against Trump found Obama ahead by double digits. (Neither man can legally be elected again.) Historical patterns are worth taking seriously, too. Every couple of decades, it seems, Democrats turn to a sunny, novel-seeming, relatively young man to resuscitate their party’s political fortunes. It happened with John F. Kennedy in 1960, Jimmy Carter in 1976, and Bill Clinton in 1992. And as Obama proved, lesser-known lawmakers with funny names can sometimes go on to make history. But there are a few reasons to wonder whether Democrats’ search for “the next Obama” has gotten a little out of hand—and might inadvertently lead the party even deeper into the political wilderness. For one thing, the comparison has become so common that it’s started to lose meaning, and power. The last few years of American politics are littered with Democrats hailed as the next Obama who ended up falling short. The late PBS anchor Gwen Ifill canonized Kamala Harris as “the female Barack Obama” back in 2009, but Harris’ two presidential campaigns both failed. A 2018 New Yorker article about the presidential prospects of Deval Patrick, the former Massachusetts governor, pronounced him “a kind of political heir to Barack Obama” and quoted former Obama advisers favorably comparing the two men. When Patrick did finally launch a 2020 campaign, it lasted just three months. Obama, of course, isn’t a ghost, and he has been talking up some of the same candidates others are likening to him. Obama called up El-Sayed last month and has publicly praised Talarico, New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani, and other promising young Democrats. Still, not even Obama’s Obamadar is perfect. In 2018, as Beto O’Rourke was weighing a national campaign, the former president was the one drawing comparisons between them. “The reason I was able to make a connection with a sizable portion of the country was because people had a sense that I said what I meant,” Obama said then, making clear that he felt O’Rourke shared that special touch. Former Obama aides reportedly called O’Rourke’s people to offer their help, remarking that the lanky Texan reminded them of their former boss. “He’s Barack Obama, but white,” one Democratic bundler told Politico. Judging by polling and fundraising, though, the rest of the country didn’t agree: O’Rourke ended up dropping out before a single primary vote was cast. That doesn’t mean Obama was wrong about their similarities. But it does suggest that giving party apparatchiks shivers of recognition isn’t enough. Another factor complicating things for the latest Obama mini-mes: Candidates themselves are starting to lean into the comparison. For them, giving off Obama vibes can be a convenient shortcut to national prominence, campaign contributions, and glowing press. But for the rest of us, it can be hard to tell whether someone is truly an Obama-caliber candidate or is just staring in the mirror a little too hard. El-Sayed, for example, used to shy away from the parallel. “There are a lot of things I disagree with President Obama on—in terms of both domestic and, certainly, international politics,” he told Vox during his 2018 run for Michigan governor. “I’m not him, and I have no intention of being him.” Last month, though, he struck a different tone. “I never saw myself in politics until I saw him give that 2004 speech on the DNC floor,” El-Sayed admiringly declared of the keynote address that propelled Obama to national stardom. El-Sayed has since doubled down, noting that Republicans have been highlighting his full name—Abdulrahman—just as they used to surround “Barack Hussein Obama” with scare quotes. (Trump, a leading proponent of the conspiracy theory that Obama was born in Africa, still does this.) Efforts to replicate the former president’s mien can also risk coming off like imitation—or, worse, parody. Shapiro’s cadence, inflection, and conspicuous use of the pregnant pause have long invited comparisons to Obama’s speaking style, not all of them positive. “It’s like if I tried to do a really bad impression of Barack Obama,” Vice President J.D. Vance has said. Shapiro is an electoral juggernaut—polls show him with an overwhelming lead in an otherwise purple state—so this might not matter much. But it has already become a punch line that could resurface if he runs for president. (“Barack Obama is the greatest orator of my lifetime,” Shapiro said when asked about the parallels in 2024, denying that he’d ever intentionally emulated the former president.) Seeing future standard-bearers through Obama-shaped glasses could also lead the party to some uncomfortable places. Since Trump’s return, candidates perceived as authentic, rough-hewn non-careerists have captured Democrats’ hearts. Obama sure has his charms, but he comes out of a different mold: the Harvard-educated lawyer who pictured himself in the Oval Office from an early age. Republicans seem to be realizing that an Obama redux can be spun into an insult—even as they also seem to be warily looking over their shoulders. When Fox News host Laura Ingraham declared Ossoff the Democrats’ “new Obama” in June, she did not mean it as a compliment. After a rally in which Ossoff made a knowing reference to Natalie Harp, a Trump White House aide with whom the president has a suspiciously close relationship, an administration spokesman blasted the freshman senator as “a cringeworthy, feminine theatre kid cosplaying as Barack Obama.” There are other downsides to the Obama-replication effort. Voters looking for a president often prize stereotypically masculine traits: Height, a deep voice, a healthy reverence for U.S. military might—all while still seeming chill enough to grab a beer with. Women can have all of those qualities, too, and political science research suggests female candidates often perform as well as or better than male ones. But it’s probably no coincidence that few women have cracked pundits’ list of Obama-like figures. Harris and Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, whose rapid ascent one Democratic strategist has called “eerily similar” to Obama’s, may be the only exceptions. Then, of course, there’s the small matter of actually beating Republicans in elections. Voters and consultants looking for an Obama-esque standard-bearer risk over-indexing on certain qualities—an inspiring backstory, an exciting policy agenda, or a smooth jump shot—while underrating others that may resonate beyond die-hard Democrats. As a candidate, Obama had the rare ability to come off like a centrist to centrists and like a progressive to progressives. But he also practiced strategic moderation, publicly opposing same-sex marriage during his first presidential run while both supporting a pathway to citizenship for immigrants who lacked permanent legal status and cracking down on unauthorized border crossings. In this way, superficial Obama similarities can mask differences that may end up being determinative in the voting booth. El-Sayed has thrilled progressives by supporting Medicare for All and trading insults with Trump—which may also help explain why he’s one of the swing-state Democrats most in danger of losing in November. (Most polls show El-Sayed leading his Republican opponent relatively narrowly even as Democrats in other purple states—including Ossoff, Shapiro, and Roy Cooper in North Carolina—appear comfortably ahead.) El-Sayed has also tolerated controversy to a degree that Obama never did. Confronted during the 2008 campaign with his longtime pastor Jeremiah Wright’s inflammatory comments, Obama cut Wright off, condemned his remarks “in unequivocal terms,” and delivered a speech that transubstantiated the controversy into a public meditation on racism and patriotism. El-Sayed, meanwhile, has repeatedly resisted calls—including from other Democrats—to disavow Hasan Piker, a left-wing streamer who has said that “America deserved 9/11.” (Piker has apologized for that one and El-Sayed called it a “dumb statement,” but Republicans are still trying their darnedest to turn it into a general-election liability.) There’s also the fact that campaign tactics and political mores have evolved since Obama’s day. The former president has sometimes spiced things up since leaving office, dropping the encomiums to America’s better angels he dispensed when he was on the ballot. At the 2024 Democratic National Convention, Obama suggested that Trump’s “weird obsession with crowd sizes” might have something to do with his anatomy. Still, Ossoff’s recent dig at Harp, which uncorked a geyser of speculation that she and the president may be having an affair, was a notably un-Obama-like move. David Axelrod, the former president’s onetime chief strategist, called it “gratuitous and unnecessary.” But Ossoff’s remark also commanded attention, feeding a boomlet that helped bump up his standing in polls of the hypothetical 2028 presidential primary. If the high horse Obama successfully rode into the White House is truly dead, Democrats who pretend otherwise risk getting outflanked. Last, all the Obama comparisons in the world won’t help Democrats figure out whether his technocratic, center-left brand of institutionalism remains the governing approach the party should adopt the next time it holds power in Washington. Obama himself has acknowledged that politics has changed since he left. “If I were running today, I wouldn’t run the same race or have the same platform as I did in 2008,” he said in 2020. The next Democratic congressional majority and the next Democratic president will have to make choices about which issues to prioritize and how far to push them: climate or healthcare, incremental progress or sweeping overhaul, policy or institutional change. In a sense, this is just the latest rejoinder in an ongoing fight. The 2020 Democratic primary was riven by debates over whether to build on Obama’s incremental progress on healthcare or start afresh with something like Medicare for All. That time, the pro-Obama wing won the argument, but Trump’s return has even moderate Democrats wondering if it’s time to try the other path. “It showed me the need for institutional change,” Buttigieg recently told NOTUS of his time working for Biden. “Democrats can’t take us back to the early 2020s or the 2010s,” he added. “We shouldn’t try.” Other Democrats weighing presidential runs are looking even further back in time. “I’m an FDR Democrat,” Rep. Ro Khanna of California said this week in New Hampshire. “I’m a Democrat who believes that this party needs to do big things again.” Amid all the hagiography, Obama’s record warrants some scrutiny. Some of his achievements, most notably the Affordable Care Act, still stand. But his presidency didn’t come close to solving structural issues that still plague the party today, from a gerrymandered House to a Senate tilted toward Republican-dominated states. And in Obama’s choices and temperament—from not passing a more generous economic stimulus and not pressuring Ruth Bader Ginsburg to step down from the Supreme Court to privately anointing Hillary Clinton as his preferred successor in 2016—there’s a case to be made that the 44th president bears some responsibility for the paroxysms of the Trump years. So if not the next Obama, who should Democrats be looking for? To answer that, it’s worth going back to seventh grade social studies. Even as my teacher whispered about him excitedly, Obama was far from the consensus choice. Back then, Democrats had a different broadly successful two-term president to look back on. Hillary wasn’t Bill; her 2008 campaign took pains to emphasize their differences. But she was literally another Clinton, and as late as January 2008 commanded nearly double Obama’s support among Democratic primary voters. Yet those same voters ended up rejecting a Clintonian retread—and holding the White House for the next eight years. There’s a clear lesson there. Ultimately, trying to clone Obama is likely to be a fool’s errand. He’s a pretty singular figure. So rather than fixating on candidates who resemble him in mannerisms or policy, Democrats might instead try to find someone who can do what he did: Give them a new way to think about politics that was so transformative that the party is still pining for it nearly 20 years later. As Obama memorably put it once upon a time, “Change will not come if we wait for some other person or if we wait for some other time. We are the ones we’ve been waiting for.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend
2026-09-08

Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. It is a mystery why President Trump continues to rely on the hapless team of Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff—his son-in-law and fellow real-estate tycoon, respectively—to hammer out treaties to end the most destructive wars of our time: Russia vs. Ukraine and Israel vs. the Palestinians. Yes, Trump doesn’t trust the professional diplomatic corps or experts of any kind. But, as a self-proclaimed businessman, he must notice when aides don’t get the job done, and the Kushner-Witkoff duo have not lured the combatants one step closer to peace; in fact, they’ve arguably intensified the war in Ukraine. Their utter incompetence was on full display over the weekend, as they met with Vladimir Putin in Moscow, then—in their first trip to the Ukrainian capital—Volodymyr Zelensky in Kyiv. They carried what Trump touted ahead of time as new peace proposals, and concluded both sets of talks with boilerplate adjectives (“productive,” “meaningful,” “positive,” “businesslike”). But in fact, they came away with zilch—and, once again, proved themselves far short of the task. This should come as no surprise. Neither has any diplomatic experience; neither knows anything about Russia or Ukraine or military strategy; nor do they travel with any specialists who might advise them along the way. Witkoff kicked off the tour with an act of full-bore genuflection toward Putin, greeting the Kremlin dictator as follows: First, Mr. President, thank you so much for having us. We were sitting outside just now, me, Jared, and Kirill [Dmitriev], and then Yuri [Ushakov] joined us, and we were all talking about when we retire, we’ll have all these incredible stories and incredible memories, and yours will be right at the top of it all. In other words, Mr. President, he might have added, we come to praise you, not pressure you into taking any actions that you don’t feel like taking. Later in the day, the aforementioned Ushakov, Putin’s senior diplomat, released a summary of the Kremlin team’s position at the meeting: The Russian army has had battlefield successes, meaning it might still win the war; Ukrainian troops must withdraw from the eastern Donbas regions; the war’s “root cause” is Zelensky’s belief that Ukraine is an independent nation; and peace will bring many business opportunities between Russia and the United States. In other words, the usual propaganda. (The U.S. envoys did not release an official “readout” of the meeting, perhaps because they are not officials. That’s right: Kushner and Witkoff hold no government posts, have not been confirmed by the Senate, and are unaccountable to anyone but Trump.) The duo then flew to Poland and from there proceeded to Kyiv by train. (Zelensky had promised not to bomb Moscow while the two were in the Kremlin, but Putin offered no such pledge in return, so the U.S. security team felt Kyiv’s airport was unsafe. In any case, Putin resumed bombarding Ukraine’s capital city shortly after the two departed. Such a good friend.) After this meeting, Zelensky insisted that the three hold a press conference, mainly with Ukrainian reporters. It did not go well. Asked if he felt “ashamed” to meet at the same table with an international war criminal like Putin, Witkoff replied: We’ve always been treated respectfully by President Putin and his team. Relationships are critical in terms of solving conflicts like this. My job, and Jared’s job, is to bring the parties together. All three sentences reflect, and reaffirm, Witkoff’s utter naivete. They have not been treated respectfully; hence the refusal to issue a hands-off order on Kyiv’s airport while the two were in town. Relationships are not critical in solving conflicts; managing interests, either through diplomacy or force, is key. (Trump, too, doesn’t understand this.) Finally, if Witkoff thinks his job is simply to bring the parties together, he doesn’t understand his job. Kushner repeated much of Witkoff’s nonsense, saying that the point of negotiations is to produce a “win-win,” including some sort of favor that fulfills Russia’s “aspirations.” Since Russia’s chief aspirations—as Putin himself has declared—are to obliterate Ukraine as an independent nation and to restore much of the old Russian Empire, this isn’t likely to be the basis for an equitable peace. Along the same lines, Kushner expressed hope that the U.S., Russia, and Ukraine could resume the trilateral talks that began shortly before Trump got sidetracked by the war on Iran (though Kushner didn’t express the chronology that way). The few talks that took place were, as he recalled, such jolly affairs. “Everyone was speaking in Russian to each other. We were discussing these issues in a very extra-friendly manner, all in Russian.” If he’d been escorted by a specialist, Kushner might have been informed that speaking Russian doesn’t make Ukrainians feel merry and welcome, especially with Russian officials who want to be their overlords. (Many Ukrainians these days regard the Russian language, including classic Russian literature, as inherently imperialist.) Another Kushner faux pas, this one perhaps reflecting his ignorance of European history: “There is no such thing in the world as forever enemies or forever allies.” He said this in the context of urging Ukrainians to imagine that they might once again be friendly with Russians. However, the analogy that some observers thought he was invoking—the U.S. alliance with Germany after World War II—was not at all fitting, as it developed only after the U.S. and other allies defeated Nazi Germany, deposed its government, tried many of its leaders for war crimes, and imposed a new constitution. Even then, it wasn’t until 10 years after the war ended that the new Federal Republic of Germany was trusted enough for the allies to invite it to join NATO. Clearly, Kushner wasn’t proposing that Putin be killed or that his government be transformed—though many in the audience might have hoped that he would. It has never been entirely clear how Trump would like this war to end—or if he thinks it should end, or whether he cares one way or the other. Until he decides, keeping Kushner and Witkoff on the case is a waste of jet fuel and time. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. WeChat worm could pwn a friend before they even answered the call
2026-09-09

WeChat worm could pwn a friend before they even answered the call

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Calif says AI helped turn a VoIP memory bug into cross-platform RCE before Tencent shut it down Tencent has patched up a zero-click vulnerability that security researchers used to create a worm capable of spreading through calls on WeChat. With more than 1.4 billion monthly active users, WeChat is among the most popular apps in the world. According to researchers at Calif, its VoIP stack contained a memory corruption bug that could enable a trusted contact to take control of a user's account simply by calling them. Calif called the flaw WeWorm, describing it as the first zero-click worm capable of spreading through WeChat calls on both iOS and Android. Calif released a demo of the vulnerability in action this week, and although Tencent has pushed fixes to address the attack on August 21, the team that found it is still withholding key details. In Calif's demonstration, the exploit took control of a victim's WeChat account within seconds, without the recipient answering the call. The compromised account then called another contact and repeated the process without user interaction. Declining the call stopped infection, but answering it or allowing it to continue ringing did not. An attacker could also try again when the recipient was away from the phone, the researchers said. "Exploitation takes only seconds, and gives us full control of the WeChat account," Calif said. "We can read and send messages, make calls, and act on the victim's behalf." The exploit requires the attacker to be on the victim's friends list. Calif argued that this offered limited protection because a compromised account could be used to target its trusted contacts. Calif said the WeWorm exploit could be chained with other vulnerabilities to compromise an entire device rather than only a WeChat account. It did not disclose the full attack chain. "Chained with other Android and iOS bugs we've reported and are helping fix, it can lead to full control of the device," the researchers said. "[Attackers] could exploit another app, gain root access using techniques like those in OEMpocalypse, take over the victim's WeChat app, and use it to attack you." Calif said it used AI to find the vulnerability and develop its first remote code execution (RCE) exploit in about two days. Tencent later confirmed the researchers' findings. The researchers said they published their high-level findings to highlight how AI could make such capabilities available beyond "well-funded, sophisticated actors." Calif plans to present the full analysis of WeWorm "at an upcoming conference." Ryan Fedasiuk, an adjunct assistant professor in Georgetown University's Security Studies Program, described the discovery of WeWorm as "an extremely serious incident." He called on the US and China to maintain open communication and share information as AI increases the potential scale and severity of cyber threats. Microsoft closes the book on Publisher and unplugs Project Online
2026-09-09

Microsoft closes the book on Publisher and unplugs Project Online

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Office 2021 joins the October retirement list as customers weigh pricier alternatives Microsoft will retire Project Online and remove Publisher from Microsoft 365 over the coming weeks, while support for perpetual versions of Publisher and for Office 2021 will end shortly afterward. Publisher, Microsoft's desktop publishing application, dates back to the Windows 3.x era. Microsoft announced its retirement in 2024 and will remove it from Microsoft 365 subscriptions on October 1, 2026. Support for perpetual versions will end on October 13 alongside Office LTSC 2021 and Consumer Office 2021. Project Online will retire on September 30. The following day, Project Online Essentials – a licensing SKU that gives users limited access to Project Online and Project Server – will reach end of life. It has not been available to new customers since October 2025. The retirement could catch out organizations that have not migrated. Essentials provides relatively inexpensive licenses for users who needed to update tasks, submit timesheets, and view project information without the broader capabilities of Microsoft's more expensive plans. Unlike Publisher, which has no direct replacement, Microsoft recommends moving Project Online Essentials users to Planner Plan 1 or Planner and Project Plan 3, depending on the capabilities and Project Server access rights they require. The additional functionality and potentially higher cost may not appeal to everyone, though. There are also third-party replacements. ProjectLibre, for example, launched ProjectLibre Cloud AI 2.0 this week. It costs just under $10 per user per month when billed annually and is one of several third-party alternatives to Microsoft's project management products. While Microsoft will not offer an Extended Security Updates program for Office 2021 after support ends, third-party provider 0patch says it will supply unofficial security fixes for critical vulnerabilities for at least another three years. Organizations still assigning Project Online Essentials licenses must move affected users to supported alternatives before October 1 to avoid interrupting their access. ® Stolen funds bought mansions, private jets, and supercars – now he faces up to 20 years Calif says AI helped turn a VoIP memory bug into cross-platform RCE before Tencent shut it down PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Office 2021 joins the October retirement list as customers weigh pricier alternatives 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Researcher believes overprivileged Iterable creds exposed 8.8M customer records – and could have enabled mass deletion See, AI can be used for good ... or at the very least, a useful distraction from the bad Multi-generation chip collab is more buzzwords than compute AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Security boffin claims airport group left API keys in client-side JavaScript for four years
2026-09-09

Security boffin claims airport group left API keys in client-side JavaScript for four years

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Researcher believes overprivileged Iterable creds exposed 8.8M customer records – and could have enabled mass deletion Security researcher Scott Helme says his analysis supports FulcrumSec's claim that Manchester Airports Group (MAG) exposed privileged API keys in client-side JavaScript. Helme says he reached that conclusion after using information provided by the cyber extortion group to reconstruct how data belonging to roughly 8.8 million MAG customers was allegedly stolen last month. The crooks behind the attack described MAG's security failure as "tragi-comical." They claimed MAG exposed overprivileged API keys for Iterable, a marketing automation platform, in front-end JavaScript served by the websites of MAG's three airports: Manchester, Stansted, and East Midlands. Helme used the Internet Archive's Wayback Machine to retrieve older versions of the JavaScript and found that the three keys first appeared across the airport websites in June and July 2022. The same values remained exposed until August 2026, he said. "Read the timeline the other way round and it's worse," said Helme. "Anyone who looked at that page source on any day between June 2022 and August 2026 could have taken the key. FulcrumSec just happen to be the ones who told us. "There is no way to know, from the outside, who else did, and the honest answer is that MAG can't know either without going back through four years of Iterable API logs, if they even have four years of Iterable API logs." The API keys were not embedded directly in the HTML, Helme explained, but anyone who examined the JavaScript bundles loaded by the sites could find them. This would explain how the vulnerability could go unnoticed by the airport's IT teams for over four years. Helme says the browser-delivered code was using the keys to authorize server-side API operations – something Iterable's documentation explicitly warns against. The requests should instead have passed through MAG's own servers, where the credentials could be kept secret and access restricted. MAG's alleged exposure of the credentials was not the only issue at play. The keys were vastly overprivileged for their intended job, which was to attribute page clicks to marketing emails, Helme said. The keys had read/write access to core Iterable endpoints, giving anyone who obtained them the ability to access data such as customer profiles, parking and lounge bookings, and Fast Track purchases. Helme said the structure and contents of the stolen data indicated that it had been exported from Iterable. He said he also found that the keys could access endpoints capable of deleting customer records and lists or rewriting profiles. "There's no indication FulcrumSec did any of this, and I'm glad, but they could have just nuked everything from orbit and MAG would have been really screwed," said Helme. "For four whole years, the capability to delete Manchester Airports Group's database was a view-source away. "This wasn't only a confidentiality exposure. It was a colossal integrity and availability exposure too, and MAG just got lucky. How do they now trust any of the data that remains in the database?" When it disclosed the incident, MAG described the cyberattack as "sophisticated" and said it was "a hack, not a lapse." The company declined to comment on Helme's conclusions. MAG is continuing its investigation alongside the Information Commissioner's Office (ICO) and supporting the National Crime Agency with its inquiries. It is understood that MAG maintains it was the victim of a crime and disputes Helme's "no hacking required" characterization. FulcrumSec released the company's data on September 2, a week after MAG confirmed it had refused to pay. According to the ICO, the group's extortion demand was lower than those typically made by cybercriminals. ® Stolen funds bought mansions, private jets, and supercars – now he faces up to 20 years Calif says AI helped turn a VoIP memory bug into cross-platform RCE before Tencent shut it down PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Office 2021 joins the October retirement list as customers weigh pricier alternatives 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Researcher believes overprivileged Iterable creds exposed 8.8M customer records – and could have enabled mass deletion See, AI can be used for good ... or at the very least, a useful distraction from the bad Multi-generation chip collab is more buzzwords than compute AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. The Verge
I spent an hour riding inside Tesla’s steering-wheel-free Cybercab
2026-09-09

I spent an hour riding inside Tesla’s steering-wheel-free Cybercab

The Verge | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
﻿Issues with the butterfly doors and bizarre drop-off locations cloud what is an otherwise impressive, future-forward experience. ﻿Issues with the butterfly doors and bizarre drop-off locations cloud what is an otherwise impressive, future-forward experience. Getting out of a taxi shouldn’t be a shameful experience. Yet I find myself feeling particularly embarrassed while exiting a Tesla Cybercab as it partially blocks a narrow road outside a popular Austin swimming hole. As a cadre of annoyed, sweaty Texan parking spot hunters pile up around it, the gold-painted two-seater just sits there obliviously, lights flashing. I try not to make eye contact, but I can feel the stares of frustrated pool-goers around me, whose earnest attempt at a peaceful outdoor reprieve from the heat had just been interrupted by yet another not-quite-finished glimpse into the future. Tesla’s long-awaited, two-seater Cybercab is officially offering paid rides in Austin, Texas, and the experience, so far, somehow feels equal parts nerve-wracking and reassuringly boring — often at the same time. The Verge spent the better part of an hour in the gilded hype-mobile last weekend, gallivanting around a roughly 288-square-mile geofenced area of the Texas capital. At its best, the service offers one of the clearest glimpses yet of a near future where autonomous vehicles feel less like a retrofitted version of the cars we know now and more like purpose-built autonomous lounges, free of steering wheels or pedals. But long wait times, reported issues with the butterfly doors, and traffic-jam-inducing drop-off locations muddy the picture. And while nothing went seriously wrong during my rides, the self-driving software also didn’t feel as confident as you would like in a car with no manual controls. It felt more like a slightly stoned teenager, nervously braking, indecisively bobbing between lanes, and seemingly second-guessing some of its decisions. There’s also an unavoidable Elon Musk–shaped cloud hanging over every ride, attracting angry eyeballs and carrying all the political baggage that comes with anything he touches. Cybercabs aren’t hard to miss, with their ostentatious gold color, Blade Runner–esque aesthetic, and dinner plate–like wheel coverings. I first saw them testing around Austin earlier this year with a human safety driver and retrofitted with steering wheel and pedals. As of today, the Texas Department of Motor Vehicles says 45 Cybercabs are registered in the state. Tesla has a lot riding on its newest vehicle. For years, Musk has hyped the imminent arrival of fully autonomous vehicles, arguing that “going balls to the wall for autonomy” was an obvious move for Tesla. The company launched its Robotaxi service last year with a fleet of self-driving Model Ys, to mixed results. But Cybercab presents a potentially much bigger bet. An entire fleet of purpose-built vehicles designed specifically to offer unsupervised driving as a service. I requested my first Cybercab ride from Tesla’s Robotaxi app around 6PM on Friday evening. It’s a good thing I didn’t have anywhere to be. The app listed an hour-plus wait time for a $15 ride under two miles, though the actual wait was about 45 minutes. (The next two wait times were about 40 and 25 minutes, respectively). The car pulled up across the street from my house, flashing under its front bumper to signal it was picking up a passenger. As someone with a less-than-stellar history with Model Y doors, I was glad the Cybercab instead let users open the butterfly-style doors by tapping a button on the mobile app. A handful of passengers have reported those same doors opening up so quickly that they end up striking them, though I never experienced that issue. The interior, a single row of two chairs separated by a cup holder, feels spacious for public transit but cramped for a private car. That’s helped somewhat by the extra legroom where a steering wheel and pedals would be. It has a sparse, minimalist gray fabric interior, save for a massive center touchscreen. That screen serves as a command center, showing a real time-3D rendering of the trip, including surrounding cars and pedestrians. It’s also how you start the ride. Once rolling, the screen doubles as an entertainment hub. I linked my Spotify, Netflix, and YouTube accounts, and tested the built-in Arcade app with an external PlayStation controller. Yes, you can game your way through a busy intersection if you really want to be that guy. The first ride took about 15 minutes. Almost immediately, the windshield wipers switched on, despite it being a sunny, 95-degree day. That wasn’t the only oddity. About halfway through the trip, the Cybercab crossed an underpass leading into downtown, a route I take almost every day. Instead of staying in the lane to go straight, it switched into one that would’ve forced a left turn onto the highway. It corrected itself, but not before a handful of cars passed by. Again, the wipers wiped. When it’s not second-guessing itself, the Cybercab’s moment-to-moment ride feels welcomely smooth. The vehicle itself is both zippy and comfortable. For a quick comparison, I requested a Waymo home and felt that, purely from a comfort standpoint, the Cybercab had a noticeable leg up. Legroom, ride quality, the app’s simplicity and usability, and in-cabin entertainment options all felt like a move in the right direction. In terms of price, fares were around the same as a typical base Uber, between $15–$21 over three trips. Riding in the Cybercab draws a great deal of attention, some endearing, some less so. Pedestrians downtown (especially kids) stopped and pointed, sometimes doing a double take at the sight of me in the driver’s seat with no wheel in front of me. One driver in the lane next to me, eyes wide, signaled for me to lower my window. When I did, he gestured with his arms and asked, “No steering wheel?” I said no pedals either, to which he responded with an emphatic, “Damn, bro!” There also aren’t any mirrors, which makes the already confined, teardrop-shaped shell feel all the more closed off, especially when sitting on the driver’s side. As someone used to unconsciously checking mirrors while driving, having them removed feels like having your peripheral vision suddenly cut off. It’s unnerving, especially with cars passing by. What’s more concerning is the Cybercab’s at times bizarre choice of drop-off spots. Several riders posted videos over the weekend of the cab stopping in the middle of busy roads, in some cases without even pulling to the curb. As mentioned above, I experienced a brief example of that after being dropped off at Austin’s popular Barton Springs. These aren’t the massive lane-closing errors that have occurred with other AVs, but they are awkward and common enough to stand out. They also won’t help win over other drivers, a majority of whom still feel uncomfortable around driverless cars. In true Tesla fashion, the Cybercab is already courting controversy. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration opened an Audit Query almost immediately after paid rides began, examining how Tesla self-certified that the car without a steering wheel or pedals complies with Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards. Competitors like Zoox, whose vehicles also lack those conventional controls, faced a similar audit years ago before ultimately pursuing and receiving a formal exemption. Tesla pressed forward with its Cybercab rollout without seeking that exemption. NHTSA said it doesn’t comment on active investigations when asked for comment by The Verge. Tesla did not respond to a request for comment. The next few weeks and months in Austin could determine how fast Tesla can expand its Cybercab vision. Musk says he intends to grow operations nationwide, and vehicles have already been spotted in Orlando and parked outside Miami International Airport. But any Cybercab crash or other high-profile mistake made in its politically friendly home state risks drawing even more regulatory scrutiny and setting back expansion timelines. And with competitors like Zoox also rolling out purpose-built AVs, delays are something Tesla really can’t afford. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

See our ethics statement. Asus’ Cetra Open wireless earbuds are some of the best that I’ve tested, despite being open-style earbuds. Their drivers sit outside of your ear, which allows outside sounds to mix with audio coming from your console. Some gamers may not want this, but it works well for me as someone who prefers to be aware of their surroundings as they play or listen to podcasts. They’re $22 off at Amazon and Best Buy, costing $206.99. In addition to their great-sounding audio and respectable battery life (plus surprisingly deep customization through a mobile or browser-based app), the Cetra Open ship with a clever USB-C 2.4GHz transmitter that has a built-in port for passthrough charging. In other words, you can charge as you play instead of forgoing a USB-C port for audio. The built-in port supports up to 60W of passthrough charging, but it can’t be used as a data port. The Cetra Open are as good of a fit for the likes of the Steam Deck, PC, Nintendo Switch 2 and PlayStation 5 as they are for your phone. They’re a breeze to connect via Bluetooth and use for music and podcasts, and there’s also a reflective band included that physically links them together behind your neck for improved visibility and stability if you want to run with them. The Download: OpenAI’s turning point for math and a battery record
2026-09-09

The Download: OpenAI’s turning point for math and a battery record

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. OpenAI says its agents have solved one of the most important open problems in mathematics. Under normal circumstances, that would be a huge milestone. But the announcement has been overshadowed by accusations that OpenAI failed to credit researchers whose AI-assisted work influenced its solution. Whether those accusations are true or not, the episode may mark a turning point in the history of mathematics. AI models now seem essential for making progress on the field’s most important problems, but solving them may demand resources available only to a couple of frontier AI companies. If that’s the future we are headed for, it is unclear how human mathematicians will fit into it. Read on to see how AI could reshape mathematics. Battery installations hit a new record in the US in the second quarter of 2026, with 20.2 gigawatt-hours of new capacity coming online. That’s enough to supply the daily electricity needs of 600,000 homes. The surge puts the country on track for another record year, driven by cheaper batteries and an urgent need for more energy storage as renewables are added to the grid. But the boom looks different for grid-scale and residential batteries. Take a closer look at the forces reshaping the US battery market. Danijar Hafner’s office in San Francisco’s SoMa district sits mostly empty, but what it lacks in decor, it makes up for in robots. Humanoids of various shapes and sizes hang like marionettes from racks that run down the center of the wide-open space. Hafner won’t say too much about his new venture just yet, but describes it as a continuation of his longtime work to enable AI to navigate environments it has not encountered in training. Over the years, the 31-year-old has honed his approach by pitting agents trained within his world models against popular video games. More recently, he’s begun migrating his agents out of the virtual world and into physical reality. Learn more about Hafner’s work teaching AI about our world. Danijar Hafner is one of the artificial intelligence honorees on our 35 Innovators Under 35 list for 2026. Meet the rest of them here, or explore the full list across the biotechnology, AI, computing and robotics, and climate and energy categories. Battlefields in Ukraine are littered with the remnants of drones. But behind all that wreckage, there’s a new gold mine for the defense sector: the data those drones generate. Ukraine has begun making millions of data points gathered during tens of thousands of drone flights available to military contractors and commercial companies. It’s a quick way to attract funding and partnerships, but it turns the front line into a model training site, using the chaos of war to create conditions that AI companies struggle to reproduce. As this new industry takes shape, we need a regulatory system that ensures battlefield data isn’t treated like ordinary commercial material. This is our latest article to become an MIT Technology Review Narrated podcast, which we publish each week on Spotify and Apple Podcasts. Batteries just broke another record in the US
2026-09-09

Batteries just broke another record in the US

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Battery installations hit a new record in the US in the second quarter of 2026. In total, 20.2 gigawatt-hours of new capacity came online, according to a new report. That’s enough to supply the daily electricity needs of about 700,000 homes. The surge is putting the country on a trajectory to see 71 gigawatt-hours of batteries installed in 2026, a 20% increase over last year. This growth is being driven by a combination of cheaper batteries and an urgent need for more energy storage capacity as renewables such as solar and onshore wind power are added to the grid. Massive, utility-scale systems are leading the way; they’re responsible for most of the record-setting quarter. Seven new gigascale battery installations (those with a capacity of over one gigawatt-hour) came online during the three-month stretch, according to the report, published by Benchmark Mineral Intelligence and the Solar Energy Industries Association. “It really came down to a handful of big projects,” says Shan Tomouk, energy storage and energy lead for Benchmark Mineral Intelligence. But there was also growth in the category of so-called behind-the-meter batteries, which include both residential and industrial battery storage systems. These projects, generally smaller than utility-scale installations, are typically owned and operated by homeowners or businesses rather than utilities or power providers. In the behind-the-meter category, data centers led the way, making up about three-quarters of new batteries in the commercial sector. But residential batteries saw a sharp slowdown. These systems are often installed in homes to store power from solar panels or serve as a backup source in case of a blackout. Home installations are projected to drop by 16% in 2026 compared with last year, according to the report. That drop happened largely because a tax credit that helped subsidize home battery systems ended in 2025, Tomouk says. Home installations should recover by the end of the decade, he adds. And tax credits for nonresidential batteries have largely survived. Overall, batteries are a bright spot in energy right now. “This is one of the strong sectors in the US,” says Isshu Kikuma, an energy storage analyst at BloombergNEF, an energy consultancy. As the battery market continues to grow, one major trend to keep an eye on is a move toward US-made technology. Today, nearly all the systems coming online use cells made in China, though some are put together into complete energy storage systems in the US. Tariffs were already pushing the US energy storage industry toward domestic production. And beginning this year, energy storage tax credits required projects to limit their reliance on batteries imported from China. There’s a lot of manufacturing capacity set to come online in the US, though these factories probably won’t be able to meet demand until at least 2030 or so, Tomouk says, so prices could tick up. A key tax credit in the US ended, but heat pump sales are still going strong. El Niño? Climate change? All of the above? Companies are developing new enrichment technology that could unlock previously overlooked resources. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. MIT Technology Review
Understanding the thermal ceiling in portable power
2026-09-09

Understanding the thermal ceiling in portable power

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Plug a phone into a modern charger and the first 10 minutes are impressive. The next 20 are not. This is not a defect. It’s the connected device protecting itself. As temperature rises during charging, a smartphone's battery management system reduces the current it will accept, because heat accelerates the chemical degradation that permanently reduces battery capacity. The charger may be capable of delivering more, but the device simply stops taking it. For anyone building products in the portable power category, this creates an uncomfortable gap between specification and experience. A device rated at 25 watts is accurate in the sense that it can deliver 25 watts. Whether it delivers 25 watts for the duration of a charge is a different question, and one the specification does not answer. The gap matters commercially because it is invisible at the point of purchase and obvious in use. Consumers compare wattage figures on packaging. They don’t compare thermal curves, because thermal curves are not published publicly. The result is a category where products differentiate on a number that describes peak output rather than sustained output, and where the actual user experience of two products with identical specifications can diverge substantially. This is particularly acute in magnetic wireless charging. Inductive power transfer generates heat at both the transmitting and receiving coils, and the magnetic attachment that makes these products convenient also places the heat source in direct contact with the device it is charging. Convenience and thermal performance are working against each other by design. The industry's response for the past several years has been materials science. Graphite sheets, thermal interface materials, conductive housings, and heat-spreading layers have all improved how efficiently accumulated heat moves away from the source. Each generation has been incrementally better than the last. But passive dissipation has a structural limitation: it can only move heat that has already been generated, and only as fast as the surrounding air will accept it. In a sealed, pocket-sized enclosure, that ceiling arrives quickly. Improving the materials slows the rate of temperature rise. It does not prevent the temperature rise. The alternative is active thermal management, which is standard in stationary electronics and largely absent from portable ones for reasons that are easy to understand. Fans add volume, weight, moving parts, and noise. In a product category defined by portability, each of those is a meaningful cost. At Anker, which manufactures charging and power products, engineering teams spent the past several development cycles working on whether that tradeoff could be made acceptable rather than eliminated. The approach involves several interacting systems: a micro centrifugal fan, dual airflow channels routed to avoid interference with the magnetic array, a three-layer graphene heat-spreading layer, and a control algorithm that modulates fan speed based on real-time temperature and battery state rather than running at a fixed rate. The result is that the Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro has become the world's fastest and coolest wireless power bank. In internal testing, at 77 °F (25 °C) ambient, the back of the power bank stays below 96.8 °F (36 °C) throughout wireless charging, 21.6 °F (12 °C) below the international standard limit of 118.4 °F (48 °C), for a comfortable grip. Comparable magnetic power banks in the same testing typically reached 113 °F (45 °C) or higher within 20 minutes. The functional consequence is that the connected device does not reach the threshold at which it begins reducing charge acceptance, so 25 watts of Qi2.2 magnetic wireless charging is delivered as a working rate rather than an opening rate. In practice, an iPhone 17 Pro reaches 50% charge in 25 minutes. The Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro’s premium performance in both charging speed and thermal management is certified by SGS, an independent testing and certification company. The same principle applies in reverse. Recharging a power bank generates heat too, which is why devices in this category are often slow to recharge, leaving users with an empty accessory at the moment they need it. Active cooling during input allows the unit to accept 45 watts and reach 80% in 52 minutes. There is a broader pattern here worth naming, because it is not unique to charging. When a category improves along a single axis for long enough, the constraint usually migrates somewhere else. Charging spent a decade optimizing power delivery. Power delivery is now, for most practical purposes, solved: the electronics can supply more energy than the receiving device is willing to accept. The binding constraint moved to thermal management, and the industry continued optimizing the axis it had always optimized, because that is the axis the specifications describe. Recognizing when a constraint has moved is difficult precisely because the old metric keeps improving. Wattage figures have continued to climb. Products have continued to get faster on paper. The measurement stayed valid while quietly ceasing to describe the thing users experience. For product organizations, the practical question is whether their specifications still measure the constraint or merely measure the capability. The two align until the constraint shifts and specifications rarely shift with it. A second implication follows from the first. If sustained performance differs meaningfully from peak performance, and if only peak performance is disclosed, then buyers cannot evaluate the products in front of them. This is one reason Anker is adding displays on charging products. The Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro shows real-time power, temperature, battery level, and estimated time remaining. Some of that is user convenience. But some of it is a Anker stating a deliberate position—this category deserves to have the complete and accurate data made transparent to all. Anker expects independent reviewers to test these claims and considers our internal numbers to be the correct outcome. The gap between specification and experience closes faster when the experience is measurable. The Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro will be available in the U.S. on September 17, 2026. This content was produced by Anker. It was not written by MIT Technology Review’s editorial staff.
